Massive twistor worldline in electromagnetic fields
Joon-Hwi Kim, Jung-Wook Kim, Sangmin Lee

Abstract
We study the (ambi-)twistor model for spinning particles interacting via electromagnetic field, as a toy model for studying classical dynamics of gravitating bodies including effects of both spins to all orders. We compute the momentum kick and spin kick up to one-loop order and show precisely how they are encoded in the classical eikonal. The all-orders-in-spin effects are encoded as a dynamical implementation of the Newman-Janis shift, and we find that the expansion in both spins can be resummed to simple expressions in special kinematic configurations, at least up to one-loop order. We confirm that the classical eikonal can be understood as the generator of canonical transformations that map the in-states of a scattering process to the out-states. We also remark that cut contributions for converting worldline propagators from time-symmetric to retarded amount to the iterated action of the leading eikonal at one-loop order.



1
Introduction

Chandrasekhar has remarked that “(t)he black holes of nature are the most perfect macroscopic objects there are in the universe: […] they are the simplest objects as well.”
Chandrasekhar:1985kt
Can we idealise these simplest objects of the universe and make them even simpler? Since electromagnetic interactions are simpler than gravitational interactions, let us phrase this question more concretely in the context of electromagnetism. What would be the description of the simplest charged, massive, spinning (macroscopic) objects moving on a background electromagnetic field?

One class of charged spinning (macroscopic) objects that can be called “simplest” is known in the literature as root-Kerr particles
Arkani-Hamed:2019ymq
, which possess spin-induced multipole moments of Kerr-Newman black holes
Chung:2019yfs
;
Scheopner:2023rzp
. They can be called simplest in the sense that they correspond to the classical spin limit of “minimal coupling” defined by the high-energy limit
Arkani-Hamed:2017jhn
, and that all multipole moments are generated by the Newman-Janis shift
Newman:1965tw
, where the position of the particle sourcing the gravitational/electromagnetic field is complexified and shifted in the imaginary spin direction. In its original formulation, the Newman-Janis shift only applies to stationary solutions of the Einstein(-Maxwell) equations, therefore the answer to the question posed in the previous paragraph would only be complete when the Newman-Janis shift is generalised to dynamical worldlines of spinning bodies.

In this work, improving upon the ideas of ref.
Guevara:2020xjx
,
we argue that the twistorial description of relativistic spherical tops
Kim:2021rda
qualifies as a complete answer.
The authors showed in ref.
Kim:2021rda
that the spherical top model
Hanson:1974qy
for a relativistic spinning particle is equivalent to a massive twistor model (similar but not identical to ref.
Fedoruk:2014vqa
) in the absence of interactions. The attempt to couple the twistor model to a background field was initiated in ref.
Kim:2023aff
.
A complete description of the twistor model minimally coupled to electromagnetic field is given here.

We use the model to compute scattering observables, such as the momentum kick and the spin kick, at low orders in perturbation theory while maintaining exact spin-dependence. Following the nomenclature of ref.
Bern:2023ccb
, we call the perturbation theory “post-Lorentzian” (PL) expansion,
where
n
-PL order terms are suppressed by
(q_{1}q_{2})^{n}
where
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
are the electric charges of two interacting particles.
When organised diagrammatically through Feynman-like diagrams,
n
-PL order terms involve
(n-1)
-loop momentum integrals, although the diagrams themselves have no loops.
This is a toy model for studying the gravitational case, where the dynamics is organised in the post-Minkowskian (PM) expansion while keeping the exact spin-dependence.

We stress that exact spin-dependence is not only of theoretical interest, but is also of phenomenological interest.
The previous sentence may sound odd to a person familiar with post-Newtonian (PN) calculations:
In the PN expansion spin effects are formally counted as 1PN, since the corrections take the form of
a/r
, and for compact objects such as black holes the spin length scales as the horizon scale
a\sim Gm
. For computing the gravitational waveforms—which are the quantities directly relevant for observations—the 4.5PN corrections seem to be good enough, at least for non-spinning quasi-circular equal mass binaries; the 4.5PN contributions add less-than-a-radian correction to the
\sim 10^{3-5}
cumulative gravitational wave cycles in the detector frequency bands
Blanchet:2023bwj
. The spin effect corrections to the conservative dynamics has already been computed to 5PN order
Kim:2021rfj
;
Kim:2022pou
;
Kim:2022bwv
;
Levi:2022dqm
;
Levi:2022rrq
;
Mandal:2022nty
;
Mandal:2022ufb
. Why would we need all-orders-in-spin effects if they are going to be smaller than what is already known, which already seems to be sufficient for observations?
1
1

1

Caution: The PN expansion is known to converge best for equal-mass quasi-circular orbits, and convergence of the best-case scenario does not guarantee convergence in other regions of the parameter space. For example, even 5PN may not be enough to reduce systematic errors below the level of statistical errors
Owen:2023mid
.

One reason all-orders-in-spin results can be of interest is because in practical applications the perturbative results need to be resummed for a better accuracy
Buonanno:1998gg
;
Antonelli:2019ytb
;
Khalil:2022ylj
;
Damour:2022ybd
;
Rettegno:2023ghr
;
Buonanno:2024vkx
.
2
2

2

One may also recall that the revival of interest in the PM expansion was partly kindled by the search for alternative resummation schemes of the gravitational two-body dynamics
Damour:2016gwp
.
The resummations reorganise the perturbative expansion by leveraging the knowledge of singularity structures that the non-perturbative answer is expected to possess.
Therefore, all-orders-in-spin calculations may reveal singularity structures we can take advantage of in the resummations, which were not visible at the lowest spin orders. This can be used, for example, in improving effective-one-body based waveform models which are known to perform worse for extremal black hole spins
Ramos-Buades:2023ehm
;
Pompili:2023tna
, where spin effects are resummed as geodesic motion on a deformed Kerr geometry
Khalil:2023kep
. Understanding the mechanism behind the effectiveness of the resummation will be useful in motivating alternative resummation schemes for spin effects, which may yield better accuracy.

From the viewpoint of resummations, an all-orders-in-spin result that is as rigid as possible and as simple as possible while keeping essential features of the dynamics will be the most useful, since we are interested in the singularity structures of all-orders-in-spin dynamics; any additional structures or free parameters may obscure the singularity structures that we wish to dig up from all-orders-in-spin results. This motivation brings us back to the question raised in the beginning of this manuscript; what is the simplest spinning object that interacts with the background Maxwell field?
The motivation also limits the inputs of the theory to all-orders-in-spin multipole moment information encoded by the Newman-Janis shift.
The expectation is that while the dynamics of the spinning particle may deviate from that of physical black holes from
\mathcal{O}(q^{2})
, the singularity structures of the all-orders-in-spin dynamics are still captured by the twistor worldline model.

The twistor worldline model predicts surprisingly simple singularity structures in special kinematic configurations, which we expect to be shared by scattering dynamics of physical black holes. For example, the 2PL
aligned-spin eikonal
(
260
) resums to the simple expression

\displaystyle\chi_{(2,\text{aligned})}

\displaystyle=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}\left(b^{2}+\frac{(\zeta-2)\gamma}{(\gamma^{2}-1)}\,\epsilon[b,v_{1},v_{2},a]+\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-\zeta)+\zeta}{\gamma^{2}-1}\,a^{2}\right)}{32\pi m_{1}\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}\,(b^{2}-a^{2})^{3/2}}+\left(1\leftrightarrow 2\right)\,,

(1)

where the impact parameter
b^{\mu}\sim(x_{1}^{\mu}-x_{2}^{\mu})_{\perp}
is defined by the covariant spin supplementary conditions (SSC),
a^{\mu}=a_{1}^{\mu}+a_{2}^{\mu}
is the sum of the spin-length vectors, and
\zeta
is the ratio parameter defined by
a_{1}^{\mu}=\zeta a^{\mu}
. To the best of authors’ knowledge, this is the first observation of spin effect resummation in binary dynamics at the next-to-leading order (NLO) in the coupling constant expansion, where the model is free of unphysical behaviour and the spins of both constituents are included to all orders.
3
3

3

The known spin-resummed NLO scattering angles reported in the literature
Guevara:2018wpp
;
Menezes:2022tcs
are based on the Compton amplitudes that develop unphysical behaviour from cubic (electromagnetism) or quintic (gravity) order in spin. The spin-resummed results reported by ref.
Brandhuber:2024bnz
should be considered as leading order effects in the
R^{3}
coupling expansion.

In addition to the construction of the interacting twistor model, and application of the model to compute observables to the 2PL order, another key result of this work is the clarification of the
classical eikonal
’s role.
In the Hamiltonian formulation of binary dynamics, the classical eikonal is defined as a suitable classical limit of the quantum eikonal phase that acts as the generator of canonical transformations, mapping the incoming scattering states to the outgoing states. The scattering states are defined in the phase space of free particles, and the eikonal encodes the interactions such that it produces all scattering observables through canonical transformations.

The manuscript is organised as follows. In section
2
we review the massive twistor model and couple it to background electromagnetic fields. In section
3
we compute scattering observables using equations of motion. We set up WQFT formulation of the model in section
4
, and use it to compute Compton amplitudes in section
5
and the classical eikonal in section
6
. We conclude our studies and propose future directions in section
7
.

Note added

While this work was being completed, ref.
Gonzo:2024zxo
appeared. Their proposal for how to extract classical observables from the radial action overlaps with our discussion on the classical eikonal as the scattering generator in section
3.1
.

2
Massive twistor in electromagnetic field

2.1
Free theory

In ref.
Kim:2021rda
, we proposed a massive twistor model
and showed its equivalence to the Hanson-Regge spherical top model at the free theory level.
A general discussion of how to couple the twistor model to background fields was given in ref.
Kim:2023aff
.
Here, we give a brief review of these two main references and clarify some aspects of the twistor model
before specialising to the minimal coupling to a background electromagnetic field.

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\mbox{\bf Spherical Top}&&&&\mbox{\bf Massive Twistor}\\
\\
\tilde{x}^{\mu},p^{\mu},\widetilde{S}^{\mu\nu},\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\mu}{}_{A=0,1,2,3}&&&&\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I},\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}},\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\\
\\
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\;\mbox{\small redundancy}\;\;\downarrow&&&&\hskip 20.00003pt\downarrow\;\;\scriptstyle{\mathrm{U(1)}}\;\mbox{\small redundancy}\\
\\
p^{\mu},\Lambda^{\mu}{}_{a=1,2,3}&&\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\;unification\;}}&&\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\bar{\lambda}_{J\dot{\alpha}}\\
x^{\mu},S^{\mu\nu}&&\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\;unification\;}}&&\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\scriptstyle{(p\cdot S\cdot\Lambda)_{a}=0=(p\cdot\Lambda)_{a}}}&&&&\end{array}

Figure 1
:
Equivalence between the spherical top model and the massive twistor model.

A widely used model for describing relativistic spinning particles is the Hanson-Regge spherical top
Hanson:1974qy
, which uses the variables
(p_{\mu},\tilde{x}^{\mu},\tilde{\Lambda}^{\mu}{}_{A},\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu})
to describe a spinning body; the momentum
p_{\mu}
, the position
\tilde{x}^{\mu}
, the body-fixed tetrad
\tilde{\Lambda}^{\mu}{}_{A}
(
A=0,1,2,3
) describing the orientation of the body, and the spin tensor
\tilde{S}_{\mu\nu}
.
The tilde notation emphasises the fact that under the “spin-gauge” redundancy
Steinhoff:2015ksa
, the variables
(\tilde{x},\tilde{\Lambda},\tilde{S})
have some gauge dependency while
p
is gauge-invariant. It was shown in ref.
Kim:2021rda
that the gauge orbit is
\mathbb{R}^{3}

and the “origin” of the orbit corresponds to the covariant gauge conditions:
p^{\mu}S_{\mu\nu}\Lambda^{\nu}{}_{a}=0=p_{\mu}\Lambda^{\mu}{}_{a}
(
a=1,2,3
).

The twistor variables describe the same dynamics with less gauge redundancy. Their global symmetry groups are the superconformal SU
(2,2)
(to be broken by the mass-shell condition) and the massive little-group SU
(2)
.
The gauge redundancy on the twistor side is U(1):
(\lambda,\mu)\rightarrow e^{i\theta}(\lambda,\mu)
,
(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\mu})\rightarrow e^{-i\theta}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\mu})
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.

The equivalence of the two models is clearly articulated in terms of the gauge-invariant coordinates on both sides.
The twistor model unifies the variables
(p_{\mu},\Lambda^{\mu}{}_{a})
using the hermitian bi-linear products of
(\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}})
:

\displaystyle p^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\,,\quad\Lambda_{(IJ)}^{\mu}=\frac{\bar{\sigma}^{\mu\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha(I}\bar{\lambda}_{J)\dot{\alpha}}}{\sqrt{2}m}\,,

(2)

in a similar vein to massive spinor-helicity variables
Conde:2016izb
;
Arkani-Hamed:2017jhn
.
An advantage of the Hamiltonian formulation is that nothing stops us from unifying momenta
p_{\mu}
and “generalised positions”
\Lambda^{\mu}{}_{a}
.
The remaining variables
(x^{\mu},S_{\mu\nu})
are unified and mapped to
a set of gauge-invariant complex variables
\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}
and their complex conjugates.
This is in contrast to the supersymmetric worldline description
Gibbons:1993ap
;
Jakobsen:2021lvp
;
Jakobsen:2021zvh

where the spin tensor
S_{\mu\nu}
is realised as a bi-linear in Grassmann variables
(\Psi_{A},\bar{\Psi}_{A})
and
the tetrad
\Lambda^{\mu}{}_{a}
is not visible.

A prominent feature of the twistor model is that there is no spin-gauge redundancy
in the first place, so that no discussion of the spin supplementary condition (SSC) is ever needed.
The only constraint yet to be imposed is the mass-shell condition
p^{2}+m^{2}=0
,
which is common to the spherical top model and the twistor model.
Figure
1
summarises the mapping between the two models before imposing the mass-shell constraint.

2.1.1
Constraints revisited

Our conventions for spinors and twistors are slightly different from those of ref.
Kim:2021rda
; see appendix
A
for details.
Let us mention a few key relations. The fundamental Poisson brackets of the spherical top model include

\displaystyle\{x^{\mu},p_{\nu}\}=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\{x^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha},p_{\beta\dot{\beta}}\}=-2\delta^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\dot{\beta}}\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\,.

(3)

The fundamental Poisson brackets of the twistor model are

\displaystyle\{\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha},\lambda_{\beta}{}^{J}\}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\delta_{I}^{J}\,,\quad\{\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I},\bar{\lambda}_{J\dot{\beta}}\}=\delta_{\dot{\beta}}^{\dot{\alpha}}\delta^{I}_{J}\,.

(4)

The incidence relations relating the two models read

\displaystyle\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}=\frac{1}{2}z^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\lambda_{\beta}{}^{I}\,,\quad\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\beta}}\bar{z}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}\,,

(5)

where the complexified position variable
z
defined by the incidence relation
is mapped to the spherical top variables through

\displaystyle z^{\mu}=x^{\mu}+iy^{\mu}\,,\quad y^{\mu}=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}S_{\rho\sigma}\,.

(6)

The imaginary part
y^{\mu}
of the complexified position variable
z^{\mu}
is related to the spin-length vector
a^{\mu}=s^{\mu}/m

widely used in the literature by
y^{\mu}=-a^{\mu}
. The extra sign is to respect the standard relation of non-relativistic spins:
\{s^{i},s^{j}\}=\epsilon^{ijk}s^{k}
.

In our current conventions, the free action of ref.
Kim:2021rda
reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&S_{\text{free}}=\int\left[\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}d\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}+\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}d\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\kappa}(\Delta-m)+\kappa(\bar{\Delta}-m)\right)d\sigma\right]\,,\\
&\Delta={\rm det}(\lambda)=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\epsilon_{IJ}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\lambda_{\beta}{}^{J}\,,\quad\bar{\Delta}={\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{IJ}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}_{J\dot{\beta}}\,.\end{split}

(7)

The Lagrange multipliers
(\kappa,\bar{\kappa})
enforce the conditions,

\displaystyle\Delta=m=\bar{\Delta}\,,

(8)

which in turn imply the mass-shell condition
-p^{2}=\Delta\bar{\Delta}=m^{2}
.
After a suitable gauge-fixing of
(\kappa,\bar{\kappa})
, the “Hamiltonian”
\mathrm{Re}(\kappa)(\Delta+\bar{\Delta})/2

generates the worldline time-evolution for all dynamical variables
which matches the expectation from the spherical top model.

Despite its success, it turns out that the free action in (
7
) is not suitable
for the transition to the interacting theory, and we
propose an alternative action:

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{free}}=\int\left[\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\dot{\bar{\mu}}_{I}{}^{\alpha}+\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\dot{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}-\kappa^{0}\phi_{0}-\kappa^{1}\phi_{1}\right]d\sigma\,,

(9)

where

\displaystyle\phi_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(m^{2}-\Delta\bar{\Delta})=\frac{1}{2}(p^{2}+m^{2})\,,\quad\phi_{1}=\frac{1}{2i}(\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{\alpha}}}\mu^{{\dot{\alpha}}I}-\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I})=p\cdot y\,.

(10)

For later purposes, we also propose the gauge-fixing conditions,

\displaystyle\chi^{0}=-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{\alpha}}}\mu^{{\dot{\alpha}}I}+\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I})\,,\quad\chi^{1}=\frac{i}{2}\log(\Delta/\bar{\Delta})\,.

(11)

We recognise
\phi_{0}
as the mass-shell constraint in twistor variables,
and
\phi_{1}
as the generator of the U(1) gauge orbit. Aside from how to implement the mass-shell constraint, the main difference between
the two proposals is that (
9
) contains
the U(1) gauge generator while (
7
) contains a U(1) gauge-fixing condition in the form

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Im}(\kappa)}{2}(\Delta-\bar{\Delta})\,.

(12)

To understand why the old proposal (
7
) is problematic and why it still yielded the free equations of motion correctly, let us revisit the general theory of constrained Hamiltonian dynamics,
building upon appendix A of ref.
Kim:2021rda
.
The system consists of Hamiltonian
H
, symplectic form
\omega
, abelian gauge generators
\phi_{A}
and gauge fixing functions
\chi^{A}
.
The minimal requirements are

\displaystyle\left\{\phi_{A},H\right\}=0,\quad\left\{\phi_{A},\phi_{B}\right\}=0\quad C^{A}{}_{B}:=\left\{\chi^{A},\phi_{B}\right\},\quad\operatorname{det}(C)\neq 0\,.

(13)

In general, the gauge-fixed action is written as

\displaystyle S=\int dt\left(p_{i}\dot{q}^{i}-H-\kappa^{A}\phi_{A}-\bar{\kappa}_{A}\chi^{A}\right)\,.

(14)

The Lagrange multipliers
(\kappa^{A},\bar{\kappa}_{A})
enforce the constraints
\phi_{A}=0=\chi^{A}
.
The variation of
S
with respect to the dynamical variables
(p_{i},q^{i})
gives the equations of motion.
The time evolution of a generic function
f(p_{i},q^{i})
is computed from the Poisson bracket

\displaystyle\frac{df}{dt}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial q^{i}}\dot{q}^{i}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial p_{i}}\dot{p}_{i}=\{f,H+\kappa^{A}\phi_{A}+\bar{\kappa}_{A}\chi^{A}\}\,.

(15)

For consistency, the equations of motion should not induce change of
\phi_{A}
and
\chi^{A}
in time:

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{A}}{dt}=0=\frac{d\chi^{A}}{dt}\,.

(16)

The vanishing of
d\phi_{A}/dt
and the minimal requirements (
13
) imply
\bar{\kappa}_{A}=0
. Hence, we do not see directly
\bar{\kappa}_{A}
in the final form of the equations of motion. The vanishing of
d\chi^{A}/dt
implies
\kappa^{A}=-(C^{-1})^{A}{}_{B}\{\chi^{B},H\}
.
We can either choose some
\chi^{A}
to fix
\kappa^{A}
, or prescribe some
\kappa^{A}
to fix
\chi^{A}
implicitly.

If some of the
\kappa^{A}
multipliers can be set to zero without violating the requirements (
13
),
the corresponding
\phi_{A}
will not directly contribute to the equations of motion and
it may look permissible to exchange the roles of
\phi_{A}
and
\chi^{A}
.
That is precisely what happened to the free twistor model.
But, as soon as we add interaction terms in the action, the distinction between the gauge generators
\phi_{A}
and
the gauge fixing conditions
\chi^{A}
becomes evident.
By the very definition of gauge redundancy, the interaction terms are required to Poisson-commute with all
\phi_{A}
in a sense to be specified below. On the contrary, there is no reason for the interaction terms to commute with
\chi^{A}
.

To conclude, in view of the general theory of constrained Hamiltonian system where the gauge generators and gauge-fixing conditions
play different roles, we need a new proposal for the free action (
9
) to incorporate interaction terms.

2.1.2
Regge trajectory

Due to rotational kinetic energy, the mass of a spinning top is in general not a constant,
but rather a function
of the spin-magnitude
W^{2}=(y\cdot p)^{2}-y^{2}p^{2}
.
The derivative
m^{\prime}=dm/d(W^{2})
is colloquially called the “Regge trajectory”.
For a free spinning particle,
the angular velocity is
(2m^{\prime})
times the spin,
so
(2m^{\prime})^{-1}
is the relativistic rotational inertia
Kim:2021rda
.

In most applications, where we do not keep track of the angular velocity and focus on the
(x,y,p)
variables, the Regge trajectory does not affect the dynamics.
Specifically,
the equations of motion for
(x,y,p)
are independent of
m^{\prime}
.
In the rest of this section, we will see, from a few different but related angles, how the Regge trajectory
m^{\prime}
decouples from the dynamics of
(x,y,p)
.
From the next subsection on, we will set
m^{\prime}=0
to simplify computations.

2.1.3
Dirac bracket and effective phase space

To describe the physical phase space of the free twistor theory,
it is convenient to construct the Dirac bracket.
The ingredients are the unconstrained Poisson bracket (
4
), the gauge generators (
10
), and the gauge-fixing functions (
11
).

Let us first consider the case of vanishing Regge trajectory (
m^{\prime}=0
). Using

\displaystyle C^{A}{}_{B}=\{\chi^{A},\phi_{B}\}=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta\bar{\Delta}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,

(17)

we construct the Dirac bracket in the standard way,

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{*}=\{f,g\}-(C^{-1})^{A}{}_{B}\left(\{f,\phi_{A}\}\{\chi^{B},g\}-\{g,\phi_{A}\}\{\chi^{B},f\}\right)\,.

(18)

The non-vanishing brackets among the twistor variables are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha},\lambda_{\beta}{}^{J}\}_{*}&=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\delta_{I}^{J}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\beta}{}^{J}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\,,\\
\{\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I},\bar{\lambda}_{J\dot{\beta}}\}_{*}&=\delta_{\dot{\beta}}^{\dot{\alpha}}\delta^{I}_{J}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\lambda}_{J{\dot{\beta}}}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{{\dot{\alpha}}I}\,,\\
\{\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha},\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}\}_{*}&=-\frac{1}{2}\left[\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}(\lambda^{-1})_{J}{}^{\beta}-\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\right]\,,\\
\{\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I},\mu^{\dot{\beta}J}\}_{*}&=-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\beta}J}-\mu^{\dot{\beta}J}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}\right]\,.\end{split}

(19)

We can certainly use these to compute the Dirac brackets among the U(1)-invariant composite variables
(x^{\mu},y^{\mu},p^{\mu})
.
But, since we are interested only in the dynamics of
(x^{\mu},y^{\mu},p^{\mu})
variables in the bulk of this paper, we can take a shortcut.

We can regard the 16-dimensional unconstrained phase space
as a fibre bundle with the base coordinates
(x,y,p)

and the fibre SU(2)
\times
U(1).
The original Poisson bracket (
4
) projected onto the
(x,y,p)
base space can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\;\;,\;\;\}_{\circ}&=\eta^{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\nu}}-\frac{y^{\mu}p^{\nu}+p^{\mu}y^{\nu}-\eta^{\mu\nu}(y\cdot p)}{p^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\nu}}\\
&\quad-\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu}[y,p]}{2p^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\mu}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\nu}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}}\right)\,.\end{split}

(20)

The transition from the Poisson bracket to the Dirac bracket
can be done within this effective description.
Since
(x,y,p)
are all U(1) gauge invariant,
we can simply disregard the pair
(\phi_{1},\chi^{1})
and take into consideration

\displaystyle\phi_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(p^{2}+m^{2})\,,\quad\chi^{0}=-x\cdot p\,.

(21)

The resulting Dirac bracket on the
(x,y,p)
base space is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\;\;,\;\;\}_{*}&=\left(\eta^{\mu\nu}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\nu}}-\frac{y^{\mu}p^{\nu}-\eta^{\mu\nu}(y\cdot p)}{p^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\nu}}\\
&\quad-\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu}[y,p]}{2p^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\mu}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\nu}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}}\right)-\frac{x^{[\mu}p^{\nu]}}{p^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}}\,.\end{split}

(22)

It agrees perfectly with the full Dirac bracket (
19
) evaluated over
(x,y,p)
.

Let us summarise the content of the effective description. The phase space is effectively nine dimensional: twelve coordinates
(x^{\mu},y^{\mu},p^{\mu})
with three constraints,

\displaystyle p^{2}+m^{2}=0\,,\quad p\cdot y=0\,,\quad p\cdot x=0\,.

(23)

By construction, the Dirac brackets of the constraints automatically vanish:

\displaystyle\{f,p^{2}+m^{2}\}_{*}=0\,,\quad\{f,p\cdot y\}_{*}=0\,,\quad\{f,p\cdot x\}_{*}=0\,.

(24)

Incidentally, the Dirac bracket is consistent with fixing the magnitude of spin:

\displaystyle\{f,y^{2}\}_{*}=0\,.

(25)

So, the phase space is eight dimensional in a sense,
although the conservation of
y^{2}
is of a dynamical origin
and shouldn’t be put on an equal footing as the true constraints.

In later sections, we will study scattering processes of the twistor particles, where the interaction is (asymptotically) turned off at the infinite past and future. It makes sense to use the (effective) free phase space for each particle to describe the asymptotic scattering states.

For completeness, we also compute the Dirac bracket
when the Regge trajectory is non-trivial (
m^{\prime}\neq 0
).
Remarkably, the
C
-matrix (
17
) is not altered by
m^{\prime}
at all.
It remains to compute the
(m^{\prime})
-dependent terms from
\{f,\phi_{0}\}\{\chi^{0},g\}
in (
18
).

In terms of the twistor variables, the function
\chi^{0}
is given by

\displaystyle\chi^{0}=-x\cdot p=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mu^{{\dot{\alpha}}I}\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{\alpha}}}+\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\right)\,.

(26)

It follows that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \{\chi^{0},\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\}&=+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\,,&\quad\{\chi^{0},\mu^{{\dot{\alpha}}I}\}&=-\frac{1}{2}\mu^{{\dot{\alpha}}I}\,,\\
\{\chi^{0},\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{\alpha}}}\}&=+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{\alpha}}}&\quad\{\chi^{0},\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\}&=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\,.\end{aligned}

(27)

The
W
tensor is defined as
Kim:2021rda

\displaystyle W_{IJ}=-i\left(\mu^{\dot{\alpha}}{}_{(I}\bar{\lambda}_{J)\dot{\alpha}}-\lambda_{\alpha(I}\bar{\mu}_{J)}{}^{\alpha}\right)\,.

(28)

It is normalized such that

\displaystyle W^{2}=\frac{1}{2}W_{K}{}^{L}W_{L}{}^{K}=(y\cdot p)^{2}-y^{2}p^{2}\,.

(29)

The
(m^{\prime})
-dependent terms from
\{f,\phi_{0}\}
come from

\displaystyle\{f,m(W^{2})^{2}/2\}=mm^{\prime}\{f,W^{2}\}\,.

(30)

It is useful to note that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \{\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},W^{2}\}&=-i\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{K}W_{K}{}^{I}\,,&\quad\{\mu^{{\dot{\alpha}}I},W^{2}\}&=-i\mu^{{\dot{\alpha}}K}W_{K}{}^{I}\,,\\
\{\bar{\lambda}_{I{\dot{\alpha}}},W^{2}\}&=+iW_{I}{}^{K}\bar{\lambda}_{K{\dot{\alpha}}}\,,&\quad\{\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha},W^{2}\}&=+iW_{I}{}^{K}\bar{\mu}_{K}{}^{\alpha}\,.\end{aligned}

(31)

The full Dirac bracket consists of two parts,

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{*}=\{f,g\}^{0}_{*}+\{f,g\}^{\prime}_{*}\,,

(32)

where
\{X,Y\}^{0}_{*}
is the result for
m^{\prime}=0
stated in (
19
)
and
\{X,Y\}^{\prime}_{*}
denotes the
(m^{\prime})
terms.
Using (
27
) and (
31
),
it is straightforward to show that the
(m^{\prime})
terms are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \{\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},\lambda_{\beta}{}^{J}\}^{\prime}_{*}&=\frac{im^{\prime}}{2m}\left[+(\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{K}W_{K}{}^{I})\lambda_{\beta}{}^{J}-\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\left(\lambda_{\beta}{}^{K}W_{K}{}^{J}\right)\right]\,,\\
\{\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},\bar{\lambda}_{J\dot{\beta}}\}^{\prime}_{*}&=\frac{im^{\prime}}{2m}\left[+(\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{K}W_{K}{}^{I})\bar{\lambda}_{J\dot{\beta}}+\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(W_{J}{}^{K}\bar{\lambda}_{K\dot{\beta}})\right]\,,\\
\{\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},\mu^{\dot{\beta}J}\}^{\prime}_{*}&=\frac{im^{\prime}}{2m}\left[-(\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{K}W_{K}{}^{I})\mu^{\dot{\beta}J}-\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(\mu^{\dot{\beta}K}W_{K}{}^{J})\right]\,,\\
\{\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I},\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}\}^{\prime}_{*}&=\frac{im^{\prime}}{2m}\left[-(\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{K}W_{K}{}^{I})\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}+\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(W_{J}{}^{K}\bar{\mu}_{K}{}^{\beta})\right]\,,\\
\{\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I},\mu^{\dot{\beta}J}\}^{\prime}_{*}&=\frac{im^{\prime}}{2m}\left[-(\mu^{\dot{\alpha}K}W_{K}{}^{I})\mu^{\dot{\beta}J}+\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\mu^{\dot{\beta}K}W_{K}{}^{I})\right]\,,\\
\{\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I},\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}\}^{\prime}_{*}&=\frac{im^{\prime}}{2m}\left[-(\mu^{\dot{\alpha}K}W_{K}{}^{I})\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}-\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}(W_{J}{}^{K}\bar{\mu}_{K}{}^{\beta})\right]\,,\end{aligned}

(33)

and their complex conjugates.
They agree perfectly with Dirac brackets computed in ref.
Kim:2021rda
despite the differences in the choice of constraints.

Comparing (
33
) with (
19
), it may appear that the non-vanishing Regge trajectory complicates
the Dirac bracket significantly.
Fortunately, when we focus on the effective
(x,y,p)
phase space, the complication disappears completely and the Dirac bracket (
22
) remains valid.
In essence, the reason is that the
(m^{\prime})
-terms in (
33
)
all involve infinitesimal shifts along the SU(2) little-group,
but the
(x,y,p)
variables are little-group scalars.

2.2
Interacting theory

In this subsection, we explain how to couple the twistor particle to a background electromagnetic field minimally using the Newman-Janis shift.

2.2.1
Symplectic perturbation theory

Given a Hamiltonian system defined by (
13
) and (
14
),
we may consider two types of deformations.
A familiar way is to deform the Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle H=H^{\circ}+q\,H^{\prime}\,,

(34)

where
q
is a continuous perturbation parameter.
We demand that the gauge generators are independent of the deformation. The requirement (
13
) implies that

\displaystyle\{\phi_{A},H^{\circ}\}=0=\{\phi_{A},H^{\prime}\}\,.

(35)

Then all the statements around (
16
) remain valid for any value of
q
.

An alternative way to deform the theory, which we adopt for our twistor model,
is to keep the Hamiltonian fixed and deform the symplectic form
Kim:2023aff
:

\displaystyle\omega=\omega^{\circ}+q\,\omega^{\prime}\,.

(36)

This is equivalent to keeping the Hamiltonian fixed and deforming the Poisson brackets used in equations of motion (EOM).
For a non-spinning particle, a key feature of the symplectic perturbation viewpoint is that
\omega^{\prime}=F=dA
is manifestly invariant under the gauge transformation of the Maxwell field and that we do not need to distinguish the two notions of momenta (canonical vs kinetic).
This feature will generalise straightforwardly to our twistor model.

Again, we demand that the gauge generators are independent of
q
.
We should make sure that the requirements
\{\phi_{A},H\}=0=\{\phi_{A},\phi_{B}\}
hold
with the deformed symplectic form. Perturbatively,

\displaystyle\{f,g\}=\{f,g\}_{\circ}-q\{f,\zeta^{m}\}_{\circ}\omega^{\prime}_{mn}\{\zeta^{n},g\}_{\circ}+\mathcal{O}(q^{2})\,,

(37)

where
\zeta^{m}
is an arbitrary coordinate system on the phase space and
\{\bullet,\bullet\}_{\circ}
is the Poisson bracket defined by
\omega^{\circ}
.

For a random choice of
\omega^{\prime}
, the perturbation term has no reason to vanish:

\displaystyle-q\{\phi_{A},\zeta^{m}\}_{\circ}\omega^{\prime}_{mn}\{\zeta^{n},\phi_{B}\}_{\circ}\neq 0\,.

(38)

But, if we change coordinates (at least locally),

\zeta^{m}\rightarrow(z^{i},w^{a})

where
z^{i}
are gauge-invariant while
w^{a}
are gauge-dependent, and take
\omega^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\omega^{\prime}_{ij}dz^{i}\wedge dz^{j}
with no
dw^{a}
components, it is immediately clear that
\{\phi_{A},H\}=0=\{\phi_{A},\phi_{B}\}
hold
exactly
.

Coming back to our twistor model, recall that the new free action (
9
) carries two gauge generators

\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
in (
10
).
Since the Poisson bracket is antisymmetric, the only issue is whether
\{\phi_{0},\phi_{1}\}=0
continues to hold
in the interacting theory.
But, as long as
\omega^{\prime}
consists only of U
(1)
-gauge invariant coordinates such as
p_{\mu}
and
z^{\mu}=x^{\mu}+iy^{\mu}
,

\{\phi_{0},\phi_{1}\}=0
would follow trivially.

A common feature of a relativistic particle theory, shared by the spherical top model, is that the mass-shell constraint
\phi_{0}=(p^{2}+m^{2})/2
also generates the worldline time-evolution.
Ignoring other gauge generators temporarily, the equation of motion of the free theory is

\displaystyle\frac{df}{d\sigma}=\kappa^{0}\{f,\phi_{0}\}_{\circ}\,.

(39)

There are two equivalent ways to express the equation of motion of the interacting theory.
One is based on the infinite series
Kim:2023aff
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\omega^{-1}&=(\omega^{\circ})^{-1}-q(\omega^{\circ})^{-1}\omega^{\prime}(\omega^{\circ})^{-1}+q^{2}(\omega^{\circ})^{-1}\omega^{\prime}(\omega^{\circ})^{-1}\omega^{\prime}(\omega^{\circ})^{-1}+\cdots\\
\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{\kappa^{0}}\frac{df}{d\sigma}&=\{f,\phi_{0}\}_{\circ}-q\{f,z^{i}\}_{\circ}\omega^{\prime}_{ij}\{z^{j},\phi_{0}\}_{\circ}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt+q^{2}\{f,z^{i}\}_{\circ}\omega^{\prime}_{ij}\{z^{j},z^{k}\}_{\circ}\omega^{\prime}_{kl}\{z^{l},\phi_{0}\}_{\circ}+\cdots\,.\end{split}

(40)

The other is more compact,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\omega^{-1}&=(\omega^{\circ})^{-1}-q(\omega^{\circ})^{-1}\omega^{\prime}\omega^{-1}\\
\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{\kappa^{0}}\frac{df}{d\sigma}&=\{f,\phi_{0}\}_{\circ}-q\{f,z^{i}\}_{\circ}\omega^{\prime}_{ij}\{z^{j},\phi_{0}\}\,,\end{split}

(41)

but less explicit in that the last term involves the Poisson bracket of the interacting theory.
Of course, (
41
) can be iterated to reproduce (
40
).

2.2.2
Minimal coupling via Newman-Janis shift

The unification of
(x^{\mu},S_{\mu\nu})
into the complex coordinates
(z,\bar{z})
defined by (
5
) and (
6
) opens up an avenue for implementing the Newman-Janis shift
Newman:1965tw
into the dynamics.
It led one of the authors to the concept of “spinspacetime”
Kim:2023vgb
which unifies spacetime and spin at the fundamental level, which has refined Newman’s early idea
Newman:1974fr
.
Our twistor model is a new member of existing attempts to incorporate the Newman-Janis shift into the dynamics
Guevara:2020xjx
;
Alessio:2023kgf
.

In the twistor model, the root-Kerr particle is defined by the minimal extension via Newman-Janis (NJ) shift from Minkowski spacetime to spinspacetime
Kim:2023aff
;
Kim:2023vgb
.
To introduce the minimal extension,
we begin with the usual coupling of a charged scalar particle to the Maxwell field
and split it into two pieces,
4
4

4

For related (but not equivalent) massive twistor models and their electromagnetic couplings, see refs.
Bette:2004ip
;
deAzcarraga:2014hda
and references therein.

\displaystyle S_{\text{int}}=q\int A_{\mu}(x)dx^{\mu}=q\int A^{+}_{\mu}(x)dx^{\mu}+q\int A^{-}_{\mu}(x)dx^{\mu}\,,

(42)

where
q
is the charge and the convention fixed by

A_{\mu}=(-\phi,\vec{A})
.
The superscripts
(\pm)
denote the self-dual and anti-self-dual parts in the sense that, under the hodge dual
*
on the field-strengths,

\displaystyle F^{\pm}=dA^{\pm}\,,\quad*F^{\pm}=\pm iF^{\pm}\,.

(43)

The minimal NJ shift correlates (anti-)self-duality and holomorphy of the complexified spacetime as
Guevara:2020xjx

\displaystyle S_{\text{int}}=q\int A^{+}_{\mu}(z)dz^{\mu}+q\int A^{-}_{\mu}(\bar{z})d\bar{z}^{\mu}=-\frac{q}{2}\int A^{+}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(z)dz^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}-\frac{q}{2}\int A^{-}_{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\bar{z})d\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\,.

(44)

In the parlance of the symplectic perturbation theory,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\omega^{\prime}&=(\omega^{\prime})^{+}+(\omega^{\prime})^{-}=\frac{q}{2}F^{+}_{\mu\nu}(z)dz^{\mu}\wedge dz^{\nu}+\frac{q}{2}F^{-}_{\mu\nu}(\bar{z})d\bar{z}^{\mu}\wedge d\bar{z}^{\nu}\,.\end{split}

(45)

Compared to the non-spinning case where
\omega^{\prime}=\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}F_{\mu\nu}(x)dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}
,
the Newman-Janis shift introduces a complicated non-linear deformation depending on the
y^{\mu}
coordinate.
But, the invariance under the Maxwell gauge transformation is still manifest.

As usual, the coupling (
44
) plays a dual role; it enters the equation of motion of the charged particle, and it acts as a source term in the Maxwell’s equations.
We focus on the former aspect in this and the next section,
leaving the latter aspect to later sections.

2.2.3
Equations of motion

Adding up the free part (
9
) and the interaction part (
44
), the full action of the root-Kerr particle becomes

\displaystyle S=\int\left[\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\dot{\bar{\mu}}_{I}{}^{\alpha}+\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\dot{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}-\frac{\kappa^{0}}{2}(m^{2}-\Delta\bar{\Delta})-\kappa^{1}W_{0}+qA^{+}_{\mu}(z)\dot{z}^{\mu}+qA^{-}_{\mu}(\bar{z})\dot{\bar{z}}^{\mu}\right]d\sigma\,.

(46)

The variation of the action with the vanishing Regge trajectory (
m^{\prime}=0
) gives

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \delta S&=\delta\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\left[\dot{\bar{\mu}}_{I}{}^{\alpha}+\frac{\kappa^{0}\Delta\bar{\Delta}}{2}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}+\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\right]\\
&\phantom{=}+\delta\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\left[\dot{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}+\frac{\kappa^{0}\Delta\bar{\Delta}}{2}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}-\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}\right]\\
&\phantom{=}+\delta\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}\left[-\dot{\bar{\lambda}}_{I\dot{\alpha}}-\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}{\bar{\lambda}}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\right]+\delta\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\left[-\dot{\lambda}_{\alpha}{}^{I}+\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}{\lambda}_{\alpha}{}^{I}\right]\\
&\phantom{=}+\delta z^{\mu}\left[qF_{\mu\nu}^{+}(z)\dot{z}^{\nu}\right]+\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}\left[qF_{\mu\nu}^{-}(\bar{z})\dot{\bar{z}}^{\nu}\right]\\
&=\delta\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}\left[\dot{\bar{\mu}}_{I}{}^{\alpha}+\frac{\kappa^{0}\Delta\bar{\Delta}}{2}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}+\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}+qF_{\mu\nu}^{+}(z)\dot{z}^{\nu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\beta\dot{\beta}}\mu^{\dot{\beta}J}(\lambda^{-1})_{J}{}^{\alpha}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\beta}\right]\\
&\phantom{=}+\delta\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\left[\dot{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}+\frac{\kappa^{0}\Delta\bar{\Delta}}{2}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}-\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}+qF_{\mu\nu}^{-}(\bar{z})\dot{\bar{z}}^{\nu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\beta\dot{\beta}}\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\beta}I}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}J}\right]\\
&\phantom{=}-\delta\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}\left[\dot{\bar{\lambda}}_{I\dot{\alpha}}+\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}{\bar{\lambda}}_{I\dot{\alpha}}+qF_{\mu\nu}^{+}(z)\dot{z}^{\nu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\right]\\
&\phantom{=}-\delta\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\left[\dot{\lambda}_{\alpha}{}^{I}-\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}+qF_{\mu\nu}^{-}(\bar{z})\dot{\bar{z}}^{\nu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{{\dot{\alpha}}I}\right]\,.\end{aligned}

(47)

In the last step, we used the incidence relations to express
\delta(z,\bar{z})
in terms of
\delta(\mu,\lambda,\bar{\mu},\bar{\lambda})
.
To specify the equations of motion completely, we have to fix the Lagrange multipliers
\kappa^{0,1}
.
A convenient choice to be used throughout this paper is

\displaystyle\kappa^{0}=\frac{1}{m}\,,\quad\kappa^{1}=0\,.

(48)

With this choice, the EOM for the twistor variables are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \dot{\bar{\mu}}_{I}{}^{\alpha}&=-\frac{m}{2}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}-qF_{\mu\nu}^{+}(z)\dot{z}^{\nu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\beta\dot{\beta}}\mu^{\dot{\beta}J}(\lambda^{-1})_{J}{}^{\alpha}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\beta}\,,\\
\dot{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}&=-\frac{m}{2}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}-qF_{\mu\nu}^{-}(\bar{z})\dot{\bar{z}}^{\nu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\beta\dot{\beta}}\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\beta}I}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}J}\,,\\
\dot{\bar{\lambda}}_{I\dot{\alpha}}&=-qF_{\mu\nu}^{+}(z)\dot{z}^{\nu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\,,\\
\dot{\lambda}_{\alpha}{}^{I}&=-qF_{\mu\nu}^{-}(\bar{z})\dot{\bar{z}}^{\nu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{{\dot{\alpha}}I}\,.\end{aligned}

(49)

The equations for the U
(1)
gauge invariant variables
(p,z,\bar{z})
are,
in the vector notation,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \dot{p}_{\mu}&=qF^{+}_{\mu\nu}(z)\dot{z}^{\nu}+qF^{-}_{\mu\nu}(\bar{z})\dot{\bar{z}}^{\nu}\,,\\
\dot{z}^{\mu}&=\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}+\frac{2iq}{m^{2}}\left[y^{\mu}p^{\nu}+p^{\mu}y^{\nu}+i\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}y_{\rho}p_{\sigma}\right]F^{-}_{\nu\lambda}(\bar{z})\dot{\bar{z}}^{\lambda}\,,\\
\dot{\bar{z}}^{\mu}&=\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}-\frac{2iq}{m^{2}}\left[y^{\mu}p^{\nu}+p^{\mu}y^{\nu}-i\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}y_{\rho}p_{\sigma}\right]F^{+}_{\nu\lambda}({z})\dot{{z}}^{\lambda}\,,\end{aligned}

(50)

where we applied the constraints
y\cdot p=0
,
\Delta\bar{\Delta}=m^{2}
after deriving the equations.
We may turn on the Regge trajectory
(m^{\prime}\neq 0)

and repeat deriving the equations.
Not surprisingly,
the twistor equations receive new terms proportional to
(m^{\prime})
,
but (
50
) remains unchanged.

The appearance of time-derivatives on the RHS of (
50
) may look peculiar.
But, it is a generic feature of the symplectic perturbation theory explained earlier. It is easy to see how (
50
) fits with the general structure of (
41
):

\displaystyle\{f,\Delta\bar{\Delta}\}=\{f,\Delta\bar{\Delta}\}_{\circ}-q\{f,z^{\nu}\}_{\circ}F^{+}_{\nu\lambda}\{z^{\lambda},\Delta\bar{\Delta}\}-q\{f,\bar{z}^{\nu}\}_{\circ}F^{-}_{\nu\lambda}\{\bar{z}^{\lambda},\Delta\bar{\Delta}\}\,.

(51)

Finally, turning on the Regge trajectory (
m^{\prime}\neq 0
) modifies (
49
) slightly, but leaves (
50
) unchanged. In what follows, we take (
50
) as the fundamental EOM for the twistor particle and use it to compute physical observables.

Unless the background takes very special values,
it would be impossible to solve the EOM exactly. We approach the problem as a perturbative expansion in
q
.
The EOM truncated up to the 2PL order is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{\mu}&=\frac{p^{\mu}}{m}-i\frac{q}{m}(F^{+}-F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y^{\nu}\\
&\quad+\frac{4q^{2}}{m^{3}}p^{\mu}(yF^{+}F^{-}y)+\frac{2q^{2}}{m^{3}}y^{\mu}\left[(pF^{+}F^{-}y)+(pF^{-}F^{+}y)\right]+\mathcal{O}(q^{3})\,,\\
\dot{y}^{\mu}&=\frac{q}{m}(F^{+}+F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y^{\nu}-i\frac{4q^{2}}{m^{3}}\left[(pF^{-}y)(F^{+})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}-(pF^{+}y)(F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\right]y^{\nu}+\mathcal{O}(q^{3})\,,\\
\dot{p}^{\mu}&=\frac{q}{m}(F^{+}+F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}p^{\nu}+\mathcal{O}(q^{3})\,.\end{split}

(52)

Here the scalar product of vectors and tensors are defined as, for example,

\displaystyle(pF^{+}F^{-}y)=p_{\mu}(F^{+})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(F^{-})^{\nu}{}_{\rho}y^{\rho}\,,\quad(pF^{-}y)=p_{\mu}(F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y^{\nu}\,.

(53)

3
Scattering observables

We compute the scattering observables (velocity kick and spin kick) of the massive spinning bodies in the conservative sector up to the 2PL order;
2PL is the lowest order where our model may disagree with other models implementing the (dynamical) Newman-Janis shift.
Before we compute the observables of our twistor model, we present a general description of the scattering observables that is valid in any (Hamiltonian) worldline model.

3.1
Classical eikonal as scattering generator

The eikonal phase has proved useful in quantum and classical scattering in field theory, gravity and string theory; see ref.
DiVecchia:2023frv
for a comprehensive review.
5
5

5

See ref.
Luna:2023uwd
for a comparison of different approaches to spinning observables beyond the leading order.

In the context of amplitudes-based methods for classical gravitational scattering, several variants of the eikonal phase are available in the literature such as the HEFT phase
Brandhuber:2021eyq
, the radial action
Bern:2021dqo
, and the exponential representation
Damgaard:2021ipf
;
Damgaard:2023ttc
just to mention a few.

Here, we introduce the notion of “classical eikonal” which is a particular classical avatar of the eikonal phase.
Simply put, the classical eikonal is the generator of the canonical transformation
between the initial states and the final states of the scattering problem.
It is motivated by the classical limit of an
S
-matrix,
but in can be defined purely within classical mechanics.
The definition is valid in any worldline model in Hamiltonian formulation.
The relevant phase space is that of free particles, just as the scattering states of a quantum scattering process are defined on the free Hilbert space.

Scattering generator

We first recall the KMOC
Kosower:2018adc
method of extracting a classical observable from a quantum theory:

\displaystyle\Delta O=\lim_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\left[\langle\psi|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{O}\hat{S}|\psi\rangle-\langle\psi|\hat{O}|\psi\rangle\right]\,.

(54)

We can trade the unitary operator
\hat{S}
for a hermitian operator
\hat{\chi}
as
Damgaard:2021ipf
;
Damgaard:2023ttc

\displaystyle\hat{S}=e^{i\hat{\chi}/\hbar}\,.

(55)

Forgetting about the state
|\psi\rangle
and working in the Heisenberg picture, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{O}^{\prime}=\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{O}\hat{S}&=e^{-i\hat{\chi}/\hbar}\hat{O}e^{i\hat{\chi}/\hbar}\\
&=\hat{O}+\frac{1}{i\hbar}[\hat{\chi},\hat{O}]+\frac{1}{2(i\hbar)^{2}}[\hat{\chi},[\hat{\chi},\hat{O}]]+\cdots\,.\end{split}

(56)

Following Dirac’s correspondence,

\displaystyle\lim_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{i\hbar}[\hat{X},\hat{Y}]=\{X,Y\}\,,

(57)

we deduce that the classical limit of (
56
) should give

\displaystyle O^{\prime}=O+\{\chi,O\}+\frac{1}{2}\{\chi,\{\chi,O\}\}+\cdots\,.

(58)

The classical quantity
\chi
, which we call “scattering generator”, is to be understood as a function on the phase space of a
Hamiltonian system.
The scattering generator defines a canonical transformation that maps a free phase space at past infinity to another free phase space at future infinity.
For a constrained Hamiltonian system with gauge generators and gauge-fixing conditions,

\chi
must be gauge-invariant. If we also demand that
O
is gauge-invariant,
the difference between the Dirac bracket and the Poisson bracket shown in (
18
) vanishes,
so we may work with the Poisson bracket (
20
).

We used the classical limit of quantum mechanics to motivate the existence of
\chi
. But, it is certainly possible to argue for its existence purely within classical mechanics. The Hamiltonian time evolution generates infinitesimal canonical transformation at each moment in time. Integrating the time evolution from past infinity to future infinity would result in a finite canonical transformation. Provided that the general relation between a Lie group and its Lie algebra holds for the (infinite dimensional) group of all canonical transformations on the free phase space, any finite canonical transformation could be written in a “conjugation” form as in (
58
).

The master formula (
58
) can compute any scattering observable.
Perturbatively, with

\displaystyle\chi=\chi_{(1)}+\chi_{(2)}+\chi_{(3)}+\cdots\,,

(59)

it produces systematically all
n
-PL impulse formulas

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{(1)}O&=\{\chi_{(1)},O\}\,,\\
\Delta_{(2)}O&=\{\chi_{(2)},O\}+\frac{1}{2}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},O\}\}\,,\\
\Delta_{(3)}O&=\{\chi_{(3)},O\}+\frac{1}{2}\{\chi_{(2)},\{\chi_{(1)},O\}\}+\frac{1}{2}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(2)},O\}\}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt+\frac{1}{6}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},O\}\}\}\,.\end{split}

(60)

An important feature of the master formula (
58
) is that, once all the constraints of the system are taken into account by a suitable Dirac bracket, the preservation of the constraints is guaranteed to all order in perturbation theory.
For the twistor model, recall from section
2.1.3
that the Dirac bracket satisfies

\displaystyle\{f,p^{2}\}_{*}=\{f,y^{2}\}_{*}=\{f,y\cdot p\}_{*}=0\,.

(61)

When
f
is gauge invariant, the Dirac bracket can be replaced by the Poisson bracket.
Setting
f=\chi=\chi_{(1)}+\chi_{(2)}+\cdots
, since
each
\chi_{(n)}
scale differently with the coupling constant, we deduce that at each
n
,

\displaystyle p\cdot\{\chi_{(n)},p\}=0\,,\quad y\cdot\{\chi_{(n)},y\}=0\,,\quad y\cdot\{\chi_{(n)},p\}+p\cdot\{\chi_{(n)},y\}=0\,.

(62)

From a perturbative point of view,
the iteration terms in (
60
) are essential for consistency.
For example, at 2PL,
the iteration term in (
60
) ensures the mass-shell condition:

\displaystyle\begin{split}2p\cdot\Delta_{(2)}p&=2p_{\mu}\{\chi_{(2)},p^{\mu}\}+p_{\mu}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},p^{\mu}\}\}\\
&=\{\chi_{(1)},p_{\mu}\{\chi_{(1)},p^{\mu}\}\}-\{\chi_{(1)},p_{\mu}\}\{\chi_{(1)},p^{\mu}\}=-(\Delta_{(1)}p)^{2}\,,\end{split}

(63)

where we used (
62
) and the Leibniz rule for the Poisson brackets.
In view of (
62
) and (
63
), when we compute the 2PL observables later in this section,
we will call
\{\chi_{(2)},O\}
and
\frac{1}{2}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},O\}\}

“transverse” and “longitudinal” (or “iteration”), respectively.

3.1.1
Non-spinning example

Arguing for the existence of
\chi
is one thing,
giving an algorithm to compute
\chi
is another.
It seems plausible that the “WQFT eikonal”
\chi_{\mathrm{WQFT}}

Mogull:2020sak
coincides with our classical eikonal
\chi
.
We will verify this expectation up to 2PL by explicit computations.
Proving the equivalence to all orders in perturbation theory is an open question, which requires incorporation of bremsstrahlung effects.

Before we compute the scattering generator and observables of the twistor model, to illustrate the ideas in a simpler setting, we review the binary dynamics of non-spinning particles in electromagnetism following ref.
Kosower:2018adc
,
which shows how to separate the transverse term
\{\chi_{(2)},p\}
from
the longitudinal term
\frac{1}{2}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},p\}\}
.
The solution of the EOM involves retarded Green functions on the worldline.
When the solution is split into the time-symmetric part and the time-anti-symmetric part,
the former gives the transverse term while the latter gives the iteration term.

The 1PL momentum kick is well known:

\displaystyle\Delta_{(1)}p_{1}^{\mu}=-q_{1}q_{2}\gamma\int_{k}(ik^{\mu})\frac{e^{ik\cdot b}}{k^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{1}\cdot k)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k)\,.

(64)

where the velocity vectors are defined as
v_{a}^{\mu}:=p_{a}^{\mu}/m_{a}

and the relative boost is defined as
\gamma:=-(v_{1}\cdot v_{2})
.
The impact parameter
b^{\mu}
is the projection of the relative position
x_{12}^{\mu}=x_{1}^{\mu}-x_{2}^{\mu}
onto the plane transverse to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.

The 1PL momentum kick (
64
) satisfies (
60
) rather trivially as

\displaystyle\Delta_{(1)}p_{1}^{\mu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial\chi_{(1)}}{\partial x_{1}^{\nu}}=\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\,,\quad\chi_{(1)}=-q_{1}q_{2}\gamma\int_{k_{\perp}}\frac{e^{ik\cdot b}}{k^{2}}\,,

(65)

where the integral over the transverse plane is defined as

\displaystyle\int_{k_{\perp}}=\int d\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt^{4}k\,\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{1}\cdot k)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k)\,.

(66)

As we proceed to the 2PL, it is useful to recall that the computation
can be organized according to the mass ratio:

\displaystyle\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{m_{1}}K^{\mu}_{1,1}+\frac{1}{m_{2}}K^{\mu}_{1,2}\,,\quad\Delta_{(2)}p_{2}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{m_{1}}K^{\mu}_{2,1}+\frac{1}{m_{2}}K^{\mu}_{2,2}\,.

(67)

The vectors
K^{\mu}_{a,b}
are integrals independent of the masses. The conservation of
(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu})
requires
that

\displaystyle K^{\mu}_{1,1}+K^{\mu}_{2,1}=0\,,\quad K^{\mu}_{1,2}+K^{\mu}_{2,2}=0\,.

(68)

The exchange symmetry between the two particles relate the integrals by

\displaystyle K^{\mu}_{1,1}\leftrightarrow K^{\mu}_{2,2}\,,\quad K^{\mu}_{1,2}\leftrightarrow K^{\mu}_{2,1}\,.

(69)

So, it suffices to compute
K^{\mu}_{1,1}
.
To do so, we may work in the probe limit
(m_{2}\rightarrow\infty)

Vines:2018gqi
.
In the rest of this section, we will work in the probe limit until further notice.

The 2PL EOM of a spinless particle in the probe limit is given by

\displaystyle\dot{v}_{(2)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{m}F^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\dot{x}_{(1)}^{\nu}+\frac{q}{m}x_{(1)}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}F^{\mu}{}_{\nu}v_{(0)}^{\nu}\,,

(70)

where
F
means
F_{(0)}
.
Using the Bianchi identity, we may rewrite it as

\displaystyle\dot{v}_{(2)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{m}(\partial^{\mu}F_{\lambda\nu})x_{(1)}^{\lambda}v_{(0)}^{\nu}+\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{q}{m}F^{\mu}{}_{\nu}x_{(1)}^{\nu}\right)\,.

(71)

The total derivative on the RHS does not contribute to the momentum kick, so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}&=q\int d\sigma(\partial^{\mu}F_{\lambda\nu})x_{(1)}^{\lambda}v_{(0)}^{\nu}\\
&=\frac{q^{2}}{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\sigma(\partial^{\mu}F_{\lambda\nu})v_{(0)}^{\nu}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma}d\sigma^{\prime}\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma^{\prime}}d\sigma^{\prime\prime}F^{\lambda}{}_{\rho}v_{(0)}^{\rho}\right]\,.\end{split}

(72)

Performing Fourier transform of (
F_{\lambda\nu}
,
F^{\lambda}{}_{\rho}
) by (
e^{i\ell\cdot x}
,
e^{ik\cdot x}
) respectively,
and switching to
q=k+\ell
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}}{m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)}{\ell^{2}(q-\ell)^{2}}(i\ell^{\mu})\left[1+\gamma^{2}\frac{\ell\cdot(q-\ell)}{(v_{1}\cdot\ell+i0^{+})^{2}}\right]\,.

(73)

The
i0^{+}
prescription comes from the retarded Green function on the worldline.
To separate the time-symmetric parts from the time-anti-symmetric parts,
as explained in ref.
Kosower:2018adc
, we apply the exchange
\ell\leftrightarrow q-\ell
to get

\displaystyle\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}}{m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)}{\ell^{2}(q-\ell)^{2}}(iq^{\mu}-i\ell^{\mu})\left[1+\gamma^{2}\frac{\ell\cdot(q-\ell)}{(v_{1}\cdot\ell-i0^{+})^{2}}\right]\,.

(74)

The next step is to take the average of (
73
) and (
74
) and
write the result as

\displaystyle\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}=\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{tr}}+\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{iter}}\,.

(75)

The time-symmetric part carries an integrand proportional to
q^{\mu}
so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{tr}}&=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}}{2m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)}{\ell^{2}(q-\ell)^{2}}(iq^{\mu})\left[1+\gamma^{2}\frac{\ell\cdot(q-\ell)}{(v_{1}\cdot\ell)^{2}}\right]\,.\end{split}

(76)

It yields the 2PL eikonal via
\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{tr}}=\{\chi_{(2)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}
.
Omitting the
i0^{+}
prescription that is no longer needed, and restoring the symmetry between the two particles,
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi_{(2)}&=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}}{2m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)}{\ell^{2}(q-\ell)^{2}}\left[1+\gamma^{2}\frac{\ell\cdot(q-\ell)}{(v_{1}\cdot\ell)^{2}}\right]+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,.\end{split}

(77)

It agrees perfectly with the WQFT eikonal
Wang:2022ntx
.
The time-anti-symmetric part of (
75
) is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{iter}}&=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}\gamma^{2}}{2m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)}{k^{2}\ell^{2}}(k\cdot\ell)\left[\frac{i\ell^{\mu}}{(v_{1}\cdot\ell+i0^{+})^{2}}-\frac{i\ell^{\mu}}{(v_{1}\cdot\ell-i0^{+})^{2}}\right]\\
&=-\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}\gamma^{2}}{2m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)}{k^{2}\ell^{2}}(k\cdot\ell)\ell^{\mu}\,\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt^{\prime}(v_{1}\cdot\ell)\,,\end{split}

(78)

where
k=q-\ell
and we used the identity,

\displaystyle\frac{i}{x+i0^{+}}-\frac{i}{x-i0^{+}}=\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(x)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{i}{(x+i0^{+})^{2}}-\frac{i}{(x-i0^{+})^{2}}=-\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt^{\prime}(x)\,.

(79)

We may compare it with the iteration term. From (
65
) and (
64
), we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\}=\frac{i}{2}(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}\left\{\gamma\int_{k_{\perp}}\frac{e^{ik\cdot b}}{k^{2}},\gamma\int_{\ell_{\perp}}\ell^{\mu}\frac{e^{i\ell\cdot b}}{\ell^{2}}\right\}\,,

(80)

In computing the bracket in (
80
),
we note that

\displaystyle\{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{1}\cdot k)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k)e^{ik\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})},\gamma\}=\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{1}\cdot k)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k)e^{ik\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\frac{ik\cdot\left(-p_{2}+p_{1}\right)}{m_{1}m_{2}}=0\,.

(81)

So, the only non-vanishing contributions come from

\displaystyle\{e^{ik\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})},\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{1}\cdot\ell)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{1}\cdot k)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k),e^{i\ell\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\}\,.

(82)

Collecting the contributions, we obtain (in the probe limit)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\}=-\frac{1}{2}(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}\gamma^{2}\int_{k,\ell}e^{i(k+\ell)\cdot b}\frac{k\cdot\ell}{k^{2}\ell^{2}}\mathcal{F}(k,\ell)\ell^{\mu}\,,\\
&\mathcal{F}(k,\ell)=\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{1}\cdot k)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k)\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt^{\prime}(v_{1}\cdot\ell)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)}{m_{1}}-(k\leftrightarrow\ell)\,.\end{split}

(83)

Using the identities,

\displaystyle\delta(x+y)\delta(y)=\delta(x)\delta(y)\,,\quad\delta(x+y)\delta^{\prime}(y)=\delta(x)\delta^{\prime}(y)-\delta^{\prime}(x)\delta(y)\,,

(84)

we can simplify (
83
) a bit further and obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1\mu}\}\}=-\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}\gamma^{2}}{2m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{k\cdot\ell}{k^{2}\ell^{2}}\ell_{\mu}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt^{\prime}(v_{1}\cdot\ell)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)\,,\end{split}

(85)

in perfect agreement with (
78
).

In the paragraphs above, we manipulated the integrands of the Fourier integrals to separate the iteration term.
In the non-spinning case, it is easy to perform the Fourier integral and compute the brackets in position space.
We write the impact parameter as

\displaystyle b^{\mu}

\displaystyle=x_{12}^{\mu}+\frac{\gamma(x_{12}\cdot v_{2})-(x_{12}\cdot v_{1})}{\gamma^{2}-1}v_{1}^{\mu}+\frac{\gamma(x_{12}\cdot v_{1})-(x_{12}\cdot v_{2})}{\gamma^{2}-1}v_{2}^{\mu}\,,\,

(86)

where
x_{12}^{\mu}=x_{1}^{\mu}-x_{2}^{\mu}
.
The 1PL non-spinning eikonal is, after the Fourier integral,

\displaystyle\chi_{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{1}q_{2}\gamma}{4\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\log\left(\frac{b^{2}}{b_{0}^{2}}\right)\right]\,,

(87)

where
\epsilon
is the dimensional regularisation parameter
D=4-2\epsilon
and
b_{0}^{2}
is a scheme-dependent constant. The precise values of
\epsilon
and
b_{0}
are irrelevant.
The relevant brackets are

\displaystyle\{b^{2},\gamma\}=0\,,\quad\{b^{2},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\doteq 2b^{\mu}\,,\quad\{b^{2},b^{\mu}\}\doteq 2b^{2}\left(\frac{\gamma v_{2}^{\mu}-v_{1}^{\mu}}{(\gamma^{2}-1)m_{1}}-\frac{\gamma v_{1}^{\mu}-v_{2}^{\mu}}{(\gamma^{2}-1)m_{2}}\right)\,,

(88)

where
\doteq
denotes equivalence up to mass-shell constraint
p_{i}^{2}+m_{i}^{2}=0
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\}&=\left[\frac{q_{1}q_{2}\gamma}{4\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}}\right]^{2}\{\log b^{2},\{\log b^{2},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\}\\
&=\left[\frac{q_{1}q_{2}\gamma}{4\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}}\frac{1}{b^{2}}\right]^{2}\{b^{2},\{b^{2},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\}\doteq 2\left[\frac{q_{1}q_{2}\gamma}{4\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}}\frac{1}{b^{2}}\right]^{2}\{b^{2},b^{\mu}\}\\
&\doteq\left[\frac{q_{1}q_{2}\gamma}{2\pi\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{b^{2}}}\right]^{2}\times\left(\frac{\gamma v_{2}^{\mu}-v_{1}^{\mu}}{(\gamma^{2}-1)m_{1}}-\frac{\gamma v_{1}^{\mu}-v_{2}^{\mu}}{(\gamma^{2}-1)m_{2}}\right)\\
&=\left|\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\right|^{2}\times\left(\frac{\gamma v_{2}^{\mu}-v_{1}^{\mu}}{(\gamma^{2}-1)m_{1}}-\frac{\gamma v_{1}^{\mu}-v_{2}^{\mu}}{(\gamma^{2}-1)m_{2}}\right)\,.\end{split}

(89)

This term generates the longitudinal impulse needed to preserve the mass-shell condition,

\displaystyle p_{1\mu}\,\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\}

\displaystyle=-\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}^{2}\,.

(90)

3.2
Momentum kick and spin kick from EOM

In the scattering problem of two spinning particles,
the momentum kick and the spin kick are the main scattering observables.
In this subsection, we compute them up to the 2PL order by solving the EOM perturbatively.
The perturbation computes the deviation from the free (straight line, constant spin) trajectory for each particle,

\displaystyle\begin{split}z^{\mu}(\sigma)&=(x_{(0)}^{\mu}+iy_{(0)}^{\mu}+v_{(0)}^{\mu}\sigma)+\delta z^{\mu}(\sigma)\,,\\
p^{\mu}(\sigma)&=m(v_{(0)}^{\mu}+\delta v^{\mu}(\sigma))\,.\end{split}

(91)

Without loss of generality, and taking constraints into consideration, we demand that the constant parameters satisfy

\displaystyle v_{(0)}\cdot v_{(0)}=-1\,,\quad(x_{(0)}+iy_{(0)})\cdot v_{(0)}=0\,.

(92)

The perturbative solution is arranged in the PL order as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta z^{\mu}&=z_{(1)}^{\mu}+z_{(2)}^{\mu}+\cdots\,,\\
\delta v^{\mu}&=v_{(1)}^{\mu}+v_{(2)}^{\mu}+\cdots\,,\end{split}

(93)

where
X_{(n)}
is proportional to
q^{n}
.
To avoid clutter, we will often omit the subscript
X_{(0)}
from background values.

3.2.1
1PL

At 1PL, the equations of motion are reduced to

\displaystyle\dot{v}_{(1)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{m}(F_{(0)}^{+}+F_{(0)}^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}v_{(0)}^{\nu}\,,

(94)

\displaystyle\dot{z}_{(1)}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=v_{(1)}^{\mu}+\frac{2iq}{m}(F_{(0)}^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y_{(0)}^{\nu}\,.

(95)

It is understood that
F^{\pm}_{(0)}
here are evaluated along the background worldline (
91
).

In computing the impulse of particle 1 to the 1PL order, we may treat particle 2 as a stationary source.
The field-strength measured at the position of particle 1 is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}F^{+}_{\mu\nu}(z_{1}(\sigma_{1}))=i\frac{q_{2}}{2}\int_{k}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k)\frac{(k\wedge v_{2})_{\mu\nu}-i\epsilon_{\mu\nu}[k,v_{2}]}{k^{2}}e^{ik\cdot(z_{1}(\sigma_{1})-\bar{z}_{2}(0))}\,,\\
F^{-}_{\mu\nu}(\bar{z}_{1}(\sigma_{1}))=i\frac{q_{2}}{2}\int_{k}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k)\frac{(k\wedge v_{2})_{\mu\nu}+i\epsilon_{\mu\nu}[k,v_{2}]}{k^{2}}e^{ik\cdot(\bar{z}_{1}(\sigma_{1})-z_{2}(0))}\,,\end{split}

(96)

where the worldline time-dependence from
z_{2}^{\mu}(\sigma_{2})=(x_{2}^{\mu}+iy_{2}^{\mu}+v_{2}^{\mu}\sigma_{2})
has been integrated out to leave
\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k)
behind.
The wedge and the epsilon notations mean

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(a\wedge b)_{\mu\nu}=a_{\mu}b_{\nu}-b_{\mu}a_{\nu}\,,\\
&\epsilon_{\mu\nu}[a,b]=\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}a^{\rho}b^{\sigma}\,,\quad\epsilon_{\mu}[a,b,c]=\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}a^{\nu}b^{\rho}c^{\sigma}\,,\quad\epsilon[a,b,c,d]=\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}a^{\mu}b^{\nu}c^{\rho}d^{\sigma}\,.\end{split}

(97)

Contracting
F^{\pm}_{\mu\nu}
with
v_{1}^{\nu}
and integrating (
94
) over
\sigma_{1}
, we obtain the velocity kick,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta_{(1)}v_{1}^{\mu}&=-\frac{q_{1}q_{2}}{2m_{1}}\int_{k_{\perp}}\frac{i\gamma k^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu}[k,v_{1},v_{2}]}{k^{2}}e^{ik\cdot(b+iy)}\\
&\qquad-\frac{q_{1}q_{2}}{2m_{1}}\int_{k_{\perp}}\frac{i\gamma k^{\mu}-\epsilon^{\mu}[k,v_{1},v_{2}]}{k^{2}}e^{ik\cdot(b-iy)}\\
&=-\frac{q_{1}q_{2}}{m_{1}}\int_{k_{\perp}}\left[(ik^{\mu})\cosh(k\cdot y)\gamma-\epsilon^{\mu}[k,v_{1},v_{2}]\sinh(k\cdot y)\right]\frac{e^{ik\cdot b}}{k^{2}}\,.\end{aligned}

(98)

The argument of the exponential is decomposed as

\displaystyle z_{1}^{\mu}-\bar{z}_{2}^{\mu}=(x_{1}^{\mu}-x_{2}^{\mu})+i(y_{1}^{\mu}+y_{2}^{\mu})=:x_{12}^{\mu}+iy^{\mu}\,,

(99)

and
x_{12}^{\mu}
is projected onto the impact parameter vector
b^{\mu}
by
\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{1}\cdot k)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot k)
. Note how the spin sum
(y_{1}^{\mu}+y_{2}^{\mu})
arises from the
difference
between complex spinspacetime coordinates.

Similarly, we can compute the spin kick and find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta_{(1)}y_{1}^{\mu}&=\frac{q_{1}q_{2}}{m_{1}}\int_{k_{\perp}}\left[i(k\wedge v_{2})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y_{1}^{\nu}\cosh(k\cdot y)-\epsilon^{\mu}[k,v_{2},y_{1}]\sinh(k\cdot y)\right]\frac{e^{ik\cdot b}}{k^{2}}\,.\end{aligned}

(100)

It is easy to verify orthogonality and conservation of magnitudes of velocity/spin at 1PL:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta(y_{1}\cdot v_{1})&=(\Delta y_{1}\cdot v_{1})+(y_{1}\cdot\Delta v_{1})=0\,,\\
\Delta(v_{1}^{2})&=2(\Delta v_{1}\cdot v_{1})=0\,,\\
\Delta(y_{1}^{2})&=2(\Delta y_{1}\cdot y_{1})=0\,.\end{aligned}

(101)

The 1PL observables can be compared with predictions of QED in the classical limit. We use the results of ref.
Bern:2023ity
as the reference. When truncated to linear order in
y
, (
98
) and (
100
) are found to be consistent with (4.45) and (4.46) of ref.
Bern:2023ity
, under the conditions
C_{i}=1
,
D_{i}=0
, and the covariant spin supplementary condition.
Note that the spin tensor kick
\Delta S^{\mu\nu}
reported by ref.
Bern:2023ity
receives contributions from both (
98
) and (
100
).

The 1PL observables can be reproduced by the classical eikonal as
Guevara:2019fsj

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{(1)}p_{1}^{\mu}&=\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}=\eta^{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}^{\nu}}\chi_{(1)}\,,\\
\Delta_{(1)}y_{1}^{\mu}&=\{\chi_{(1)},y_{1}^{\mu}\}=\frac{1}{m_{1}}\left[v_{1}^{\mu}y_{1}^{\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}^{\nu}}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu}[v_{1},y_{1}]\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}^{\nu}}\right]\chi_{(1)}\,.\end{split}

(102)

An explicit form of the 1PL eikonal is

\displaystyle\chi_{(1)}

\displaystyle=-q_{1}q_{2}\int_{k_{\perp}}\left[\cosh(k\cdot y)\gamma-i\frac{\sinh(k\cdot y)}{k\cdot y}\epsilon[k,v_{1},v_{2},y]\right]\frac{e^{ik\cdot b}}{k^{2}}\,.

(103)

To reproduce (
98
) and (
100
) from (
103
) via (
102
),
we need the identity,

\displaystyle\frac{k^{\mu}\epsilon[k,v_{1},v_{2},y]}{k\cdot y}\approx-\epsilon^{\mu}[k,v_{1},v_{2}]\,.

(104)

The approximate equality (
\approx
) means that we may impose the transversality condition
k\cdot v_{1}=0=k\cdot v_{2}
and ignore ultra-local (
\propto k^{2}/k^{2}
) terms inside the integral.
With this understanding, the identity above can be derived from the 4d Schouten identity,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&a^{\mu}\epsilon[b,c,d,e]+b^{\mu}\epsilon[c,d,e,a]+c^{\mu}\epsilon[d,e,a,b]\\
&\qquad+d^{\mu}\epsilon[e,a,b,c]+e^{\mu}\epsilon[a,b,c,d]=0\,.\end{split}

(105)

In what follows, we will not distinguish the approximate equality from the strict equality.
Inside the integrands of the Fourier integrals, we will take the liberty to set

\displaystyle k^{2}=0\,,\quad\ell^{2}=0\,,\quad k\cdot v_{2}=0=\ell\cdot v_{2}\,,\quad(k+\ell)\cdot v_{1}=0\,.

(106)

3.2.2
2PL

The 2PL equations directly relevant for the impulse computation are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{v}_{(2)}^{\mu}&=\frac{q}{m}(F^{+}_{(0)}+F^{-}_{(0)})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}v_{(1)}^{\nu}+\frac{q}{m}(F^{+}_{(1)}+F^{-}_{(1)})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}v_{(0)}^{\nu}\,,\\
\dot{y}_{(2)}^{\mu}&=\frac{q}{m}(F^{+}_{(0)}+F^{-}_{(0)})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y_{(1)}^{\nu}+\frac{q}{m}(F^{+}_{(1)}+F^{-}_{(1)})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y_{(0)}^{\nu}\\
&\quad-i\frac{4q^{2}}{m^{2}}\left[(vF^{-}y)(F^{+})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}-(vF^{+}y)(F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\right]_{(0)}y_{(0)}^{\nu}\,.\end{split}

(107)

2PL velocity kick

As we saw in the non-spinning case,
it is sufficient to work in the probe limit
(m_{1}/m_{2}\rightarrow 0)
,
and we can use the Bianchi identity and discard a total derivative to get

\displaystyle\Delta_{(2)}p^{\mu}

\displaystyle=q\int d\sigma(\partial^{\mu}F^{+}_{\lambda\nu})z_{(1)}^{\lambda}v_{(0)}^{\nu}+q\int d\sigma(\partial^{\mu}F^{-}_{\lambda\nu})\bar{z}_{(1)}^{\lambda}v_{(0)}^{\nu}\,.

(108)

Next, using (
95
), we can turn
(z_{(1)},\bar{z}_{(1)})
into integrals,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{(2)}p^{\mu}&=\Delta_{(2v)}p^{\mu}+\Delta_{(2y)}p^{\mu}\,,\\
\Delta_{(2v)}p^{\mu}&=\frac{q^{2}}{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\sigma\partial^{\mu}(F^{+}+F^{-})_{\lambda\nu}v_{(0)}^{\nu}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma}d\sigma^{\prime}\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma^{\prime}}d\sigma^{\prime\prime}(F^{+}+F^{-})^{\lambda}{}_{\rho}v_{(0)}^{\rho}\right]\,,\\
\Delta_{(2y)}p^{\mu}&=\frac{2iq^{2}}{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\sigma(\partial^{\mu}F^{+}_{\lambda\nu})v^{\nu}_{(0)}\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma}d\sigma^{\prime}(F^{-})^{\lambda}{}_{\rho}y_{(0)}^{\rho}\\
&\quad-\frac{2iq^{2}}{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\sigma(\partial^{\mu}F^{-}_{\lambda\nu})v^{\nu}_{(0)}\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma}d\sigma^{\prime}(F^{+})^{\lambda}{}_{\rho}y_{(0)}^{\rho}\,.\end{split}

(109)

We divided the computation into two parts. The “y-part” (
\Delta_{(2y)}p^{\mu}
) is linear in
y_{1}
aside from the
y
-dependence in the exponential factors. The “v-part” (
\Delta_{(2v)}p^{\mu}
) is independent of
y_{1}
aside from the exponential factors. While we replace
v^{\mu}_{(1)}
by
\dot{z}_{(1)}
or
\dot{\bar{z}}_{(1)}
, we also encounter terms proportional to
(F^{+})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(F^{-})^{\nu}{}_{\lambda}-(F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(F^{+})^{\nu}{}_{\lambda}
, but they vanish identically.
Let us analyse the two parts one by one.

For the y-part, after using the field-strengths (
96
) and integrating over the worldline, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{(2y)}p_{1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}}{m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)(i\ell^{\mu})}{\ell^{2}k^{2}(ik\cdot v_{1}+0^{+})}\left(\mathrm{ch}\!\boxminus C_{y}+\mathrm{sh}\!\boxminus S_{y}\right)\,,

(110)

where we set
q=k+\ell
as before and introduce shorthand notations,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{ch}\boxminus=\cosh[(k-\ell)\cdot y]\,,&\quad\mathrm{sh}\boxminus=\sinh[(k-\ell)\cdot y]\,,\\
\mathrm{ch}\boxplus=\cosh[(k+\ell)\cdot y]\,,&\quad\mathrm{sh}\boxplus=\sinh[(k+\ell)\cdot y]\,.\end{split}

(111)

The functions
C_{y}
,
S_{y}
are

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{y}&=-\epsilon[k,\ell,y_{1},v_{1}]+2(v_{2}\cdot y_{1})\epsilon[k,\ell,v_{1},v_{2}]\,,\\
S_{y}&=i(\ell\cdot v_{1})[(k-\ell)\cdot y_{1}]+2i\gamma(v_{2}\cdot y_{1})(k\cdot\ell)\,.\end{split}

(112)

We divide the v-part further into the same helicity contribution (
\Delta_{(2vs)}p^{\mu}
)
and the opposite helicity contribution (
\Delta_{(2vo)}p^{\mu}
).
The opposite helicity part is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{(2vo)}p_{1}^{\mu}&=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}}{m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)(i\ell^{\mu})}{\ell^{2}k^{2}(ik\cdot v_{1}+0^{+})^{2}}\left(\mathrm{ch}\!\boxminus\,C_{vo}+\mathrm{sh}\!\boxminus\,S_{vo}\right)\,,\\
&\quad C_{vo}=-(\gamma^{2}-\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}})(k\cdot\ell)+(k\cdot v_{1})(\ell\cdot v_{1})\,,\quad S_{vo}=i\gamma\,\epsilon[k,l,v_{1},v_{2}]\,.\end{split}

(113)

The same helicity part is quite simple:

\displaystyle\Delta_{(2vs)}p_{1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}}{m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)(i\ell^{\mu})}{\ell^{2}k^{2}(ik\cdot v_{1}+0^{+})^{2}}(-\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}})(k\cdot\ell)\mathrm{ch}\!\boxplus\,.

(114)

In the non-spinning limit
(y\rightarrow 0)
, it cancels against the
+\frac{1}{2}(k\cdot\ell)
term of
C_{vo}
in (
113
),
in agreement with (
73
).

2PL eikonal

As in the non-spinning example, we can extract the 2PL eikonal from the 2PL momentum kick.
The key idea
Kosower:2018adc
is to apply the exchange
\ell\leftrightarrow k=q-\ell
to the integrand,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}=\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}(i\ell^{\mu})\,\mathcal{J}(k,\ell)=\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}(iq^{\mu}-i\ell^{\mu})\,\mathcal{J}(\ell,k)\,.\end{split}

(115)

Since
q\cdot v_{1}=k\cdot v_{1}+\ell\cdot v_{1}=0
,
the exchange flips the
i0^{+}
prescription for the worldline Green function.
Taking the average of the two expressions and taking the term proportional to
q^{\mu}
in the integrand,
we separate the transverse part of the momentum kick, from which
we read off the 2PL eikonal equipped with the time-symmetric
i0^{+}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{tr}}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}(iq^{\mu})\,\mathcal{J}(\ell,k)=\{\chi_{(2)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}=\eta^{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}_{1}}\chi_{(2)}\\
&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\chi_{(2)}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\mathcal{J}(\ell,k)\,.\end{split}

(116)

The remaining
\ell^{\mu}
terms in the integrand should be matched against the iteration term,

\displaystyle\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{iter}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}(i\ell^{\mu})\,\left[\mathcal{J}(k,\ell)-\mathcal{J}(\ell,k)\right]=\frac{1}{2}\{\chi_{(1)},\{\chi_{(1)},p_{1}^{\mu}\}\}\,.

(117)

We have computed
\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}
from the EOM. It is straightforward to split it into
\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{tr}}
and
\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{iter}}
,
and then read off
\chi_{(2)}
from
\left.\Delta_{(2)}p_{1}^{\mu}\right|_{\mathrm{tr}}
.
The final result for
\chi_{(2)}
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi_{(2)}&=\chi_{(2)1}+\chi_{(2)2}\,,\\
\chi_{(2)1}&=-\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}}{2m_{1}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)}{k^{2}\ell^{2}(\ell\cdot v_{1})^{2}}\left[\mathcal{V}_{1}+\mathcal{Y}_{1}\right]\,,\\
\mathcal{V}_{1}&=\left[-(\gamma^{2}-\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}})(k\cdot\ell)+(k\cdot v_{1})(\ell\cdot v_{1})\right]\cosh[(k-\ell)\cdot y]\\
&\quad-\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}(k\cdot\ell)\cosh[(k+\ell)\cdot y]+i\gamma\,\epsilon[k,\ell,v_{1},v_{2}]\sinh[(k-\ell)\cdot y]\,,\\
\mathcal{Y}_{1}&=-i(\ell\cdot v_{1})\left[-\epsilon[k,\ell,y_{1},v_{1}]+2(v_{2}\cdot y_{1})\epsilon[k,\ell,v_{1},v_{2}]\right]\cosh[(k-\ell)\cdot y]\\
&\quad+(\ell\cdot v_{1})\left[(\ell\cdot v_{1})[(k-\ell)\cdot y_{1}]+2\gamma(v_{2}\cdot y_{1})(k\cdot\ell)\right]\sinh[(k-\ell)\cdot y]\,.\end{split}

(118)

The other half of the answer,
\chi_{(2)2}
, can be obtained from
\chi_{(2)1}
by the exchange of particle labels
(1\leftrightarrow 2)
.
Verifying the iteration relation (
117
) is also straightforward, but involves a lengthy computation; see appendix
C
for details.

2PL spin kick

To complete the 2PL story, let us compute the 2PL spin kick from the EOM and confirm that it can be reproduced by the 2PL eikonal we obtained earlier.
We begin with a copy of the 2PL EOM for spin from (
107
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{y}_{(2)}^{\mu}&=\frac{q}{m}(F^{+}_{(0)}+F^{-}_{(0)})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y_{(1)}^{\nu}+\frac{q}{m}(F^{+}_{(1)}+F^{-}_{(1)})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y_{(0)}^{\nu}\\
&\quad-i\frac{4q^{2}}{m^{2}}\left[(vF^{-}y)(F^{+})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}-(vF^{+}y)(F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\right]_{(0)}y_{(0)}^{\nu}\,.\end{split}

(119)

Using the 1PL EOM, we turn the terms on the RHS to integrals,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{(2)}y^{\mu}&=\Delta_{(2a)}y^{\mu}+\Delta_{(2b)}y^{\mu}+\Delta_{(2c)}y^{\mu}+\Delta_{(2d)}y^{\mu}\,,\\
\Delta_{(2a)}y^{\mu}&=\frac{q^{2}}{m^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\sigma(F^{+}+F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma}d\sigma^{\prime}(F^{+}+F^{-})^{\nu}{}_{\lambda}y_{(0)}^{\lambda}\,,\\
\Delta_{(2b)}y^{\mu}&=\frac{q^{2}}{m^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\sigma\partial_{\lambda}(F^{+}+F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y_{(0)}^{\nu}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma}d\sigma^{\prime}\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma^{\prime}}d\sigma^{\prime\prime}(F^{+}+F^{-})^{\lambda}{}_{\rho}v_{(0)}^{\rho}\right]\,,\\
\Delta_{(2c)}y^{\mu}&=\frac{2iq^{2}}{m^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\sigma\partial_{\lambda}(F^{+})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y_{(0)}^{\nu}\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma}d\sigma^{\prime}(F^{-})^{\lambda}{}_{\rho}y_{(0)}^{\rho}\\
&\quad-\frac{2iq^{2}}{m^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\sigma\partial_{\lambda}(F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}y_{(0)}^{\nu}\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma}d\sigma^{\prime}(F^{+})^{\lambda}{}_{\rho}y_{(0)}^{\rho}\,,\\
\Delta_{(2d)}y^{\mu}&=-i\frac{4q^{2}}{m^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\sigma\left[(vF^{-}y)(F^{+})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}-(vF^{+}y)(F^{-})^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\right]_{(0)}y_{(0)}^{\nu}\,.\end{split}

(120)

Both (b) and (c) terms come from the second term on the RHS of (
119
).

Again, it suffices to work in the probe limit; we keep using the Fourier integral (
96
