) of the field-strength produced by a fixed source.
After worldline time integrals, we reach an expression of the form

\displaystyle\Delta_{(2)}y_{1}^{\mu}=\frac{(q_{1}q_{2})^{2}}{m_{1}^{2}}\int_{q_{\perp}}e^{iq\cdot b}\int_{\ell}\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(v_{2}\cdot\ell)}{k^{2}\ell^{2}(ik\cdot v_{1}+0^{+})^{2}}\mathcal{N}^{\mu}\,.

(121)

The numerator
\mathcal{N}^{\mu}
can be computed separately for each term in (
120
).
For (a) and (b) terms,
we also distinguish the same/opposite helicity contributions.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{N}^{\mu}_{(2ao)}=\left(\mathrm{ch}\!\boxminus C_{ao}+\mathrm{sh}\!\boxminus S_{ao}\right)^{\mu}\,,&\quad\mathcal{N}^{\mu}_{(2as)}=\left(\mathrm{ch}\!\boxplus C_{as}+\mathrm{sh}\!\boxplus S_{as}\right)^{\mu}\,,\\
\mathcal{N}^{\mu}_{(2bo)}=\left(\mathrm{ch}\!\boxminus C_{bo}+\mathrm{sh}\!\boxminus S_{bo}\right)^{\mu}\,,&\quad\mathcal{N}^{\mu}_{(2bs)}=\left(\mathrm{ch}\!\boxplus C_{bs}+\mathrm{sh}\!\boxplus S_{bs}\right)^{\mu}\,,\\
\mathcal{N}^{\mu}_{(2c)}=\left(\mathrm{ch}\!\boxminus C_{c}+\mathrm{sh}\!\boxminus S_{c}\right)^{\mu}\,,&\quad\mathcal{N}^{\mu}_{(2d)}=\left(\mathrm{ch}\!\boxminus C_{d}+\mathrm{sh}\!\boxminus S_{d}\right)^{\mu}\,,\end{split}

(122)

It is straightforward to evaluate all
C
,
S
functions in (
122
).
The intermediate steps involve many terms, but after some cancellations, the final results are often quite simple.
For instance, the same helicity sector gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}C^{\mu}_{as}+C^{\mu}_{bs}&=-\frac{i}{2}(k\cdot\ell)\left[v^{\mu}_{1}(\ell\cdot y_{1})-y^{\mu}_{1}(\ell\cdot v_{1})\right]\,,\\
S^{\mu}_{as}+S^{\mu}_{bs}&=-\frac{1}{2}(k\cdot\ell)\epsilon^{\mu}[v_{1},y_{1},\ell]\,.\end{split}

(123)

After collecting and simplifying the terms, using the symmetrisation of the
i0^{+}
prescription,
we extract the transverse part of the spin kick and check whether the result agrees with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\chi_{(n)},y_{1}^{\mu}\}&=\frac{1}{m_{1}}\left[v_{1}^{\mu}y_{1}^{\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}^{\nu}}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu}[v_{1},y_{1}]\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}^{\nu}}\right]\chi_{(n)}\,.\end{split}

(124)

Again, the procedure is straightforward, and we confirm the agreement, but the calculations tend to be lengthy. We give some details in appendix
C.2
.

4
Twistor WQFT

Worldline Quantum Field Theory (WQFT)
Mogull:2020sak
is a means of organising classical equations of motion in a diagrammatic way that resembles Feynman diagrams of particle physics.
The simplest WQFT model action consists of two parts; the bulk part which describes field degrees of freedom (DOFs) on the background spacetime, and one-dimensional sigma model that takes the background spacetime as the target space, where the latter is interpreted as the worldline action of a point particle moving on the background spacetime. The DOFs are decomposed into the background value (which satisfies the classical equations of motion) and fluctuations from the background value, and diagrammatic techniques developed for perturbative QFT calculations are applied to the field fluctuations. The background value for the background spacetime is usually taken to be the flat Minkowski spacetime, and the background value for the worldline is usually taken to be the straight trajectory of a free particle;
x^{\mu}(\sigma)=b^{\mu}+v^{\mu}\sigma+\delta x^{\mu}(\sigma)
. The fluctuation DOFs are evaluated as a perturbative series of the coupling constant, which in the gravitational case is taken to be the Newton’s constant
G
.

4.1
Worldline Feynman rules

When we apply the WQFT method to our twistor model, one novelty is that the twistor variables
(\lambda,\bar{\lambda},\mu,\bar{\mu})
are the fundamental variables, and the target space of the worldline is the twistor space indirectly related to the background spacetime by the incidence relations.
It is natural to express the propagators and vertex factors in terms of the twistor variables.
But, it is often convenient to use the vector variables
(z,\bar{z})
in intermediate steps.
We will use the incidence relations to switch between the twistor variables (“twistor picture”)
and the vector variables (“spacetime picture”) whenever necessary.

Classical limit

When
\hbar
is restored, the fundamental variables have the dimensions

\displaystyle[\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}]=[\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}]=[M^{1/2}]\,,\,[\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}]=[\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}]=[M^{1/2}L]\,,\,[A_{\mu}]=[M^{1/2}L^{-1/2}]\,,

(125)

and the coupling becomes dimensionful:
[q]=[M^{1/2}L^{1/2}]
. The action also becomes
iS\to iS/\hbar
, therefore the interaction vertices are weighted by
\hbar^{-1}
while the propagators (both
\langle\lambda\bar{\mu}\rangle
type and
\langle\mu\mu\rangle
type) are weighted by
\hbar
. We have no other
\hbar
scaling if we only use frequency
\omega
and wavenumber four-vector
k^{\mu}
in momentum space, which is allowed because there is no “mass” in any of the fluctuation fields. Therefore, at a given
q
order (which determines the number of vertices coupling to the photon field), the classical contribution is determined from the diagrams with the least number of propagators that makes the diagram connected, which is equivalent to the statement that tree diagrams determine the classical physics. The tree diagrams, however, generally have momentum integrals similar to loop integrals of quantum field theory.

4.1.1
Background-fluctuation expansion

In the non-spinning WQFT, the expansion around a straight line trajectory is done by

\displaystyle x^{\mu}(\sigma)=b_{0}^{\mu}+v^{\mu}\sigma+\delta x^{\mu}(\sigma)\,,\quad v^{2}=-1\,,\quad b_{0}\cdot v=0\,.

(126)

After the NJ-shift, the expansion is generalised as

\displaystyle z^{\mu}(\sigma)=b_{0}^{\mu}+iy_{0}^{\mu}+v^{\mu}\sigma+\delta z^{\mu}(\sigma)\,,\quad y_{0}\cdot v=0\,.

(127)

We should rephrase the background-fluctuation expansion in terms of twistor variables.
As we explained in section
2.1.2
, we may assume a flat Regge trajectory
m^{\prime}=0
.
The resulting equations of motion for the free action (
9
) are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{\delta S_{\text{free}}}{\delta\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}}&=0=-\frac{d\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}}{d\sigma}+\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}{\lambda}_{\alpha}{}^{I}\\
\frac{\delta S_{\text{free}}}{\delta{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}}&=0=-\frac{d\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}}{d\sigma}-\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}{\bar{\lambda}}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\\
\frac{\delta S_{\text{free}}}{\delta{\lambda}_{\alpha}{}^{I}}&=0=\frac{d\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}}{d\sigma}+\frac{\kappa^{0}\Delta\bar{\Delta}}{2}(\lambda^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}+\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\\
\frac{\delta S_{\text{free}}}{\delta\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}}&=0=\frac{d{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}}{d\sigma}+\frac{\kappa^{0}\Delta\bar{\Delta}}{2}(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}-\frac{\kappa^{1}}{2i}{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}\,.\end{aligned}

(128)

Just like in the previous section, we fix the Lagrange multipliers as
\kappa^{0}=1/m
and
\kappa^{1}=0
. We introduce the background values
\ell_{\alpha}{}^{I}
and
\bar{\ell}_{I\dot{\alpha}}
satisfying the conditions

\displaystyle{\rm det}(\ell)={\rm det}(\bar{\ell})=m\,,\quad\ell_{\alpha}{}^{I}\bar{\ell}_{I\dot{\alpha}}=-mv_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\,,

(129)

where
v^{\mu}
is the normalised velocity vector introduced in
(
127
).
The following relations satisfied by inverse matrices are useful in calculations.

\displaystyle(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}=-\frac{\epsilon_{IJ}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\ell_{\beta}{}^{J}}{{\rm det}(\ell)}\,,\quad(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}=\frac{\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{IJ}\bar{\ell}_{J\dot{\beta}}}{{\rm det}(\bar{\ell})}\,,\quad(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}=-\frac{v^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}}{m}\,.

(130)

The twistor variables are expanded as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}&\to\ell_{\alpha}{}^{I}+\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(\sigma)\,,\\
\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}&\to\bar{\ell}_{I\dot{\alpha}}+\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)\,,\\
\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}&\to c^{\dot{\alpha}I}-\frac{m}{2}(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}\sigma+\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\sigma)\,,\\
\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}&\to\bar{c}_{I}{}^{\alpha}-\frac{m}{2}(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\sigma+\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}(\sigma)\,.\end{aligned}

(131)

The background-fluctuation expansion for
z^{\mu}
and
\bar{z}^{\mu}
can be determined from the incidence relations (
274
),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} z^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}&\to+2c^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}-m(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\sigma+\delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\sigma)=z_{0}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}+v^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\sigma+\delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\sigma)\,,\\
\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}&\to+2(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}\bar{c}_{I}{}^{\alpha}-m(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\sigma+\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\sigma)=\bar{z}_{0}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}+v^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\sigma+\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\sigma)\,.\end{aligned}

(132)

The relation between the fluctuation fields are determined from the incidence relations.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 2\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\sigma)&=\left(z_{0}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}+v^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\sigma\right)\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(\sigma)+\delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\sigma)\ell_{\alpha}{}^{I}+\delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\sigma)\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(\sigma)\,,\\
2\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}(\sigma)&=\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)\left(\bar{z}_{0}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}+v^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\sigma\right)+\bar{\ell}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\sigma)+\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}(\sigma)\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\sigma)\,.\end{aligned}

(133)

We use the positive frequency expansion,

\displaystyle\phi(x)=\int_{k}\phi(k)e^{ikx}\,,\quad f(\sigma)=\int_{\omega}f(\omega)e^{-i\omega\sigma}\,,

(134)

which relates the frequency space coefficients to annihilation modes and incoming momenta. The frequency space expression for the incidence relation turns out to be more useful

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega)&=\left[2\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}-z_{0}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\lambda_{\beta}{}^{I}+iv^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\frac{\partial\lambda_{\beta}{}^{I}}{\partial\omega}-\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}(\omega^{\prime})\lambda_{\beta}{}^{I}(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\right](\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\,,\\
\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega)&=(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}\left[2\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}-\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\beta}}\bar{z}_{0}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}+i\frac{\partial\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\beta}}}{\partial\omega}v^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}-\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\beta}}(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\right]\,,\end{aligned}

(135)

where we have suppressed the
\omega
dependence whenever it is obvious.

The free action in terms of the fluctuation fields becomes

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} S_{\text{free}}&=\int\left[\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}d\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}+\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}d\mu^{\dot{\alpha}I}+\frac{{\rm det}(\lambda)+{\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})+m(\ell^{-1})^{\alpha}{}_{I}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}J}\bar{\lambda}_{J\dot{\alpha}}}{2}d\sigma\right.\\
&\phantom{=}\left.\phantom{\frac{asdf}{2}}+\frac{{\rm det}(\lambda)(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}+(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}{\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})}{2}d\sigma+\frac{{\rm det}(\lambda){\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})}{2m}d\sigma+\cdots\right]\,.\end{aligned}

(136)

where the ellipsis denotes constant and total derivative terms irrelevant for Feynman rules. The first line determines the 2pt functions, while the second line generates cubic and quartic vertices.

4.1.2
Twistor propagators

The quadratic part of the free action in frequency space can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} iS_{2}&=\frac{i}{2}\int_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(\omega^{\prime})&\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})&\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}(\omega^{\prime})&{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\omega^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}\\
&\phantom{=a}\times\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\epsilon_{IJ}&-i\omega\delta_{I}^{J}\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}&\frac{m}{2}(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\beta}J}&0\\
+i\omega\delta^{I}_{J}\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}&0&0&0\\
\frac{m}{2}(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\ell^{-1})_{J}{}^{\beta}&0&+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{IJ}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}&-i\omega\delta^{I}_{J}\delta^{\dot{\alpha}}_{\dot{\beta}}\\
0&0&+i\omega\delta_{I}^{J}\delta_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\dot{\beta}}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{\beta}{}^{J}(\omega)\\
\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}(\omega)\\
\bar{\lambda}_{J\dot{\beta}}(\omega)\\
{\mu}^{\dot{\beta}J}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}\,,\end{aligned}

(137)

where we used the delta support to convert
\omega^{\prime}\to-\omega
. Inverting the quadratic action leads to the twistor propagators
in the straight line background:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\lambda_{\alpha}^{~I}(\omega^{\prime})\bar{\mu}_{J}^{~\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}\delta_{J}^{I}}{\omega}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\quad\langle\bar{\mu}_{I}^{~\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\bar{\mu}_{J}^{~\beta}(\omega)\rangle=+\frac{i}{2}\frac{\epsilon_{IJ}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}(\omega^{\prime}){\mu}^{\dot{\beta}J}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{\delta_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\dot{\beta}}\delta_{I}^{J}}{\omega}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\quad\langle{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\omega^{\prime}){\mu}^{\dot{\beta}J}(\omega)\rangle=-\frac{i}{2}\frac{\epsilon^{IJ}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}}{\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\omega^{\prime})\bar{\mu}_{J}^{~\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{im}{2}\frac{(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\ell^{-1})^{\beta}{}_{J}}{\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,.\end{aligned}

(138)

An
i0^{+}
prescription is needed to determine the causality flow of the 2pt functions; e.g. for
\omega\to\omega+i0^{+}
causality flows from
\omega^{\prime}
to
\omega
. Otherwise stated, we use time-symmetric
i0^{+}
prescription in the calculations, which is the prescription relevant for computing the eikonal
Jakobsen:2021zvh
. We also remark that all position type 2pt functions (i.e.
\langle\mu\mu\rangle
,
\langle\bar{\mu}\bar{\mu}\rangle
, and
\langle\mu\bar{\mu}\rangle
) should be understood as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\bar{\mu}_{I}^{~\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\bar{\mu}_{J}^{~\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=+\frac{i\kappa^{0}{\rm det}(\bar{\ell})}{2}\frac{\epsilon_{IJ}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\omega^{\prime}){\mu}^{\dot{\beta}J}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{i\kappa^{0}{\rm det}(\ell)}{2}\frac{\epsilon^{IJ}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}}{\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\omega^{\prime})\bar{\mu}_{J}^{~\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{i\kappa^{0}{\rm det}(\ell){\rm det}(\bar{\ell})}{2}\frac{(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\ell^{-1})^{\beta}{}_{J}}{\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\end{aligned}

(139)

when we remove the gauge-fixing condition
\kappa^{0}=\frac{1}{m}
and background value determinant conditions
{\rm det}(\ell)={\rm det}(\bar{\ell})=m
.
This will become relevant in discussions of causality cuts applied to the twistor model
Kim:causality-cut
.

Let us introduce a graphical notation for the propagators (
138
).
Without the mass-shell constraint, the worldline propagator would be simply

\fmffile{worldline-propagator-unconstrained}\parbox{80.0pt}{\fmfgraph*(50,50)
\fmfstraight\fmfbottom{i1,o1}
\fmftop{i2,o2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=empty,decor.size=10,label=$Z_{A}{}^{I}\;$,label.angle=180}{v2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=shaded,decor.size=10,label=$\bar{Z}_{J}{}^{B}\;$,label.angle=180}{v1}
\fmf{phantom}{i1,v1,o1}
\fmf{phantom}{i2,v2,o2}
\fmf{plain,tension=0.4}{v1,v2}
}\hskip-42.67912pt=\;\;-\frac{\delta_{A}{}^{B}\delta_{J}{}^{I}}{\omega}\,,

(140)

which includes
\langle\lambda\bar{\mu}\rangle
and
\langle\bar{\lambda}\mu\rangle
propagators.
The mass-shell constraint introduces additional propagators. We denote the
\langle\mu\mu\rangle
,
\langle\bar{\mu}\bar{\mu}\rangle

propagators as

\fmffile{worldline-propagator-constrained}\parbox{80.0pt}{\fmfgraph*(50,50)
\fmfstraight\fmfbottom{i1,o1}
\fmftop{i2,o2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=empty,decor.size=10,label=$\mu^{{\dot{\alpha}}I}\;$,label.angle=180}{a2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=empty,decor.size=10,label=$\mu^{{\dot{\beta}}J}\;$,label.angle=180}{a1}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=full,decor.size=5}{t0}
\fmf{phantom}{i1,a1,o1}
\fmf{phantom}{i2,a2,o2}
\fmf{plain,tension=0.4}{a1,t0,a2}
}\hskip-42.67912pt=\;\;-\frac{i}{2}\frac{\epsilon^{IJ}\epsilon^{{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}}{\omega^{2}}\,,\hskip 28.45274pt\parbox{80.0pt}{\fmfgraph*(50,50)
\fmfstraight\fmfbottom{i1,o1}
\fmftop{i2,o2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=shaded,decor.size=10,label=$\bar{\mu}_{I}{}^{\alpha}\;$,label.angle=180}{a2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=shaded,decor.size=10,label=$\bar{\mu}_{J}{}^{\beta}\;$,label.angle=180}{a1}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=full,decor.size=5}{t0}
\fmf{phantom}{i1,a1,o1}
\fmf{phantom}{i2,a2,o2}
\fmf{plain,tension=0.4}{a1,t0,a2}
}\hskip-42.67912pt=\;\;+\frac{i}{2}\frac{\epsilon_{IJ}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{\omega^{2}}\,.

(141)

The black dots in the middle remind us of the fact that these propagators originate from the
(\bar{\lambda}\bar{\lambda})

and
(\lambda\lambda)
vertices in the Lagrangian.
6
6

6

This is a valid interpretation of the 2pt functions; we may only regard (
140
) as the fundamental 2pt functions and understand the position type 2pt functions, (
141
) and (
142
), as insertions of 2pt vertices between products of fundamental 2pt functions. See appendix
D
for regularisation of the divergences related to the symmetric
i0^{+}
prescription of the 2pt functions.

Finally, we denote the
\langle\mu\bar{\mu}\rangle
propagator by

\fmffile{worldline-propagator-last}\parbox{80.0pt}{\fmfgraph*(50,50)
\fmfstraight\fmfbottom{i1,o1}
\fmftop{i2,o2}

\fmf{phantom}{i1,a1,o1}
\fmf{phantom}{i2,a2,o2}

}\hskip-42.67912pt=\;\;-\frac{im}{2}\frac{(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}(\ell^{-1})^{\beta}{}_{J}}{\omega^{2}}\,.

(142)

The black square in the middle is to show that this propagator comes from the

m(\ell^{-1})(\bar{\ell}^{-1})
vertex in the Lagrangian.

4.1.3
Vector 2-point functions

Turning to the “spacetime picture” where we organize diagrams
in terms of
{\delta}z
,
{\delta}\bar{z}
,
the following 2pt functions will play a crucial role.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\delta z^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{2i\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{m\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{2i\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{m\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\delta z^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{2}{m}\left[\left(\frac{iv^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}v^{\dot{\beta}\beta}}{\omega^{2}}+\frac{v^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}z_{0}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}}{\omega^{\prime}}+\frac{v^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\bar{z}_{0}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}}{\omega}\right)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\right.\\
&\phantom{=}\left.\phantom{\frac{v^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}z_{0}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}}{\omega^{\prime}}}-iv^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}v^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{\omega^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\omega}\right)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\right]\,,\end{aligned}

(143)

where
\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt^{\prime}(x)=\frac{d}{dx}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(x)
is the delta derivative. Note that terms proportional to
\omega^{-2}
in (
143
) are contributions from the 2pt functions given in (
139
).
We have neglected the loop contribution to the
\langle\delta\bar{z}\delta z\rangle
2pt function,

\displaystyle\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\delta z^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega)\rangle\supset-\frac{4v^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}v^{\dot{\beta}\beta}}{m^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\int_{\omega_{1}}\frac{1}{\omega_{1}(\omega-\omega_{1})}\to 0\,,

(144)

based on two reasons. First,
\hbar
counting from dimensional analysis requires an extra
\hbar
factor for this loop contribution compared to tree contributions given in (
143
). Second, the loop integral evaluates to zero if we assume invariance under shifts of the integration variable:
7
7

7

Although widely used in dimensional regularisation, this is not a trivial assumption; for example, ABJ anomalies evaluate to zero under this assumption for (divergent) loop integrals.

\displaystyle\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\omega^{\prime}(\omega-\omega^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{\omega}\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\left[\frac{1}{\omega^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{\omega^{\prime}-\omega}\right]\,.

(145)

The delta derivative contribution can be simplified by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left(\frac{1}{\omega^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\omega}\right)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)&=\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left[\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)}{\omega^{\prime}}\right]+\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega^{\prime}}\left[\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)}{\omega}\right]\\
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left[-\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)}{\omega}\right]+\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega^{\prime}}\left[\frac{\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)}{\omega}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\end{aligned}

(146)

leading to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\delta z^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{2}{m}\left[\frac{i(v^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}v^{\dot{\beta}\beta}-v^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}v^{\dot{\beta}\alpha})}{\omega^{2}}+\frac{v^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}(\bar{z}_{0}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}-z_{0}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha})}{\omega}\right]\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\\
&=-\frac{2}{m}\left[\frac{i\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{\omega^{2}}+\frac{v^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}(\bar{z}_{0}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}-z_{0}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha})}{\omega}\right]\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\end{aligned}

(147)

so that the delta derivative contribution vanishes.
We have used
v^{2}=-1
to simplify the second line;
v^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}v^{\dot{\beta}\beta}-v^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}v^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}=-v^{2}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}
.
Note that the term proportional to
\omega^{-1}
implies propagation of spin degrees of freedom
y^{\mu}\propto z^{\mu}-\bar{z}^{\mu}
.

In the vector notation, the
\delta z
and
\delta\bar{z}
2pt functions take the following form,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\delta z^{\mu}(\omega^{\prime})\delta z^{\nu}(\omega)\rangle&=\frac{i\eta^{\mu\nu}}{m\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}(\omega^{\prime})\delta\bar{z}^{\nu}(\omega)\rangle&=\frac{i\eta^{\mu\nu}}{m\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}(\omega^{\prime})\delta z^{\nu}(\omega)\rangle&=\frac{i}{m}\left[\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}}{\omega^{2}}+\frac{2(v^{\mu}y_{0}^{\nu}+y_{0}^{\mu}v^{\nu}+i\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}v_{\lambda}y_{0\sigma})}{\omega}\right]\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,.\end{aligned}

(148)

It is useful to present the 2-point functions pictorially.
To distinguish them from the twistor propagators (
140
)-(
142
), we denote
\delta z^{\mu}
,
\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}
by squares:

\fmffile{zz-or-zbarzbar-2pt}\parbox{80.0pt}{\fmfgraph*(50,50)
\fmfstraight\fmfbottom{i1,o1}
\fmftop{i2,o2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=square,decor.filled=empty,decor.size=10,
label=$\delta z^{\mu}\;$,label.angle=180}{a2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=square,decor.filled=empty,decor.size=10,
label=$\delta z^{\nu}\;$,label.angle=180}{a1}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=full,decor.size=0}{t0}
\fmf{phantom}{i1,a1,o1}
\fmf{phantom}{i2,a2,o2}
\fmf{plain,tension=0.4}{a1,t0,a2}
}\hskip-45.52458pt=\;\langle\delta z^{\mu}\delta z^{\nu}\rangle\,,\hskip 28.45274pt\parbox{80.0pt}{\fmfgraph*(50,50)
\fmfstraight\fmfbottom{i1,o1}
\fmftop{i2,o2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=square,decor.filled=shaded,decor.size=10,
label=$\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}\;$,label.angle=180}{a2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=square,decor.filled=shaded,decor.size=10,
label=$\delta\bar{z}^{\nu}\;$,label.angle=180}{a1}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=full,decor.size=0}{t0}
\fmf{phantom}{i1,a1,o1}
\fmf{phantom}{i2,a2,o2}
\fmf{plain,tension=0.4}{a1,t0,a2}
}\hskip-45.52458pt=\;\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}\delta\bar{z}^{\nu}\rangle\,,\hskip 28.45274pt\parbox{80.0pt}{\fmfgraph*(50,50)
\fmfstraight\fmfbottom{i1,o1}
\fmftop{i2,o2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=square,decor.filled=shaded,decor.size=10,
label=$\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}\;$,label.angle=180}{a2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=square,decor.filled=empty,decor.size=10,
label=$\delta\bar{z}^{\nu}\;$,label.angle=180}{a1}
\fmfv{decor.shape=circle,decor.filled=full,decor.size=0}{t0}
\fmf{phantom}{i1,a1,o1}
\fmf{phantom}{i2,a2,o2}
\fmf{plain,tension=0.4}{a1,t0,a2}
}\hskip-45.52458pt=\;\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}\delta z^{\nu}\rangle\,.

(149)

Finally, separating the position
x
and the spin-length
y
,
we get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\delta x^{\mu}(\omega^{\prime})\delta x^{\nu}(\omega)\rangle&=\left[\frac{i\eta^{\mu\nu}}{m\omega^{2}}-\frac{1}{m\omega}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}v_{\lambda}y_{0\sigma}\right]\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle\delta y^{\mu}(\omega^{\prime})\delta y^{\nu}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{1}{m\omega}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}v_{\lambda}y_{0\sigma}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle\delta x^{\mu}(\omega^{\prime})\delta y^{\nu}(\omega)\rangle&=\frac{1}{m\omega}\left(v^{\mu}y_{0}^{\nu}+y_{0}^{\mu}v^{\nu}\right)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)=-\langle\delta y^{\mu}(\omega^{\prime})\delta x^{\nu}(\omega)\rangle\,.\end{aligned}

(150)

4.1.4
Higher order correlators

For computations at 2PL or higher orders, we will need to evaluate the higher order correlators. We write the 2pt correlators as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\delta z^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{2i\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{m\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{2i\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{m\omega^{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\delta z^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{2i}{m}\left(\frac{\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{\omega^{2}}-\frac{2v^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}y_{0}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}}{\omega}\right)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,.\end{aligned}

(151)

The connected part of the higher point correlators can be computed using the recursive substitutions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega)&\to\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\delta z^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}(\omega^{\prime})\times\left[-\lambda_{\beta}{}^{I}(\omega-\omega^{\prime})(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\right]\,,\\
\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega)&\to\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\left[-(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\beta}}(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\right]\times\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\,,\end{aligned}

(152)

the 2pt correlators

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\langle\lambda_{\beta}{}^{I}(\omega^{\prime})\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\gamma}\gamma}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{2v^{\dot{\gamma}\alpha}\delta_{\beta}^{\gamma}}{m\omega}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
-(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}\langle\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\beta}}(\omega^{\prime})\delta{z}^{\dot{\gamma}\gamma}(\omega)\rangle&=-\frac{2v^{\dot{\alpha}\gamma}\delta_{\dot{\beta}}^{\dot{\gamma}}}{m\omega}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\end{aligned}

(153)

additional vertices from the free action

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} iS_{\text{free},3}&=\frac{i}{2}\int d\sigma\left[{\rm det}(\lambda)(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}+(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}{\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})\right]\\
&=\frac{i}{2}\int_{\omega^{\prime},\omega}\left((\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}(\omega^{\prime}){\rm det}(\lambda)[\omega]+(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(\omega^{\prime}){\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})[\omega]\right)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\\
iS_{\text{free},4}&=\frac{i}{2m}\int d\sigma\left[{\rm det}(\lambda){\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})\right]=\frac{i}{2m}\int_{\omega^{\prime},\omega}{\rm det}(\lambda)[\omega^{\prime}]{\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})[\omega]\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega^{\prime}+\omega)\,,\end{aligned}

(154)

where

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\rm det}(\lambda)[\omega]&=-\frac{\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\epsilon_{IJ}}{2}\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\lambda_{\alpha}{}^{I}(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\lambda_{\beta}{}^{J}(\omega^{\prime})\,,\\
{\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})[\omega]&=\frac{\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{IJ}}{2}\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\bar{\lambda}_{J\dot{\beta}}(\omega^{\prime})\,,\end{aligned}

(155)

and determinant insertions to the 2pt correlators

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\!\langle\delta{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega_{0})\delta{z}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega_{1}){\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})[\omega_{2}]\rangle\!\rangle&=\frac{4\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{m\omega_{0}\omega_{1}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,,\\
\langle\!\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega_{0})\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega_{1}){\rm det}({\lambda})[\omega_{2}]\rangle\!\rangle&=\frac{4\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{m\omega_{0}\omega_{1}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,.\end{aligned}

(156)

For example, the following 3pt correlator can be computed as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\!\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega_{0})\delta{z}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega_{1})\delta{z}^{\dot{\gamma}\gamma}(\omega_{2})\rangle\!\rangle&=-{(\bar{\ell}^{-1})^{\dot{\alpha}I}}\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\langle\bar{\lambda}_{I\dot{\delta}}(\omega_{0}-\omega^{\prime})\delta{z}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega_{1})\rangle\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\delta}\alpha}(\omega^{\prime})\delta{z}^{\dot{\gamma}\gamma}(\omega_{2})\rangle\\
&\phantom{=}-{(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\beta}}\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\langle\lambda_{\delta}{}^{I}(\omega_{1}-\omega^{\prime})\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega_{0})\rangle\langle\delta{z}^{\dot{\beta}\delta}(\omega^{\prime})\delta{z}^{\dot{\gamma}\gamma}(\omega_{2})\rangle\\
&\phantom{=asdf}+\left(\omega_{1}\leftrightarrow\omega_{2}\,,\,\beta\leftrightarrow\gamma\,,\,\dot{\beta}\leftrightarrow\dot{\gamma}\right)\\
&\phantom{=}+\frac{i}{2}\int_{\omega^{\prime}}{(\ell^{-1})_{I}{}^{\delta}}\langle\lambda_{\delta}{}^{I}(-\omega^{\prime})\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega_{0})\rangle\\
&\phantom{=asdfasdfasdf}\times\langle\!\langle\delta{z}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega_{1})\delta{z}^{\dot{\gamma}\gamma}(\omega_{2}){\rm det}(\bar{\lambda})[\omega^{\prime}]\rangle\!\rangle\,,\end{aligned}

where the first three lines come from the expansion (
152
) and the last line comes from the insertion (
156
). The result partially simplifies to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\!\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega_{0})\delta{z}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega_{1})\delta{z}^{\dot{\gamma}\gamma}(\omega_{2})\rangle\!\rangle&=-\frac{4iv^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}}{m^{2}}\left(\frac{\epsilon^{\dot{\beta}\dot{\gamma}}\epsilon^{\alpha\gamma}}{\omega_{0}\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}+\frac{2v^{\dot{\beta}\gamma}y_{0}^{\dot{\gamma}\alpha}}{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}\right)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\\
&\phantom{=asdf}+\left(\omega_{1}\leftrightarrow\omega_{2}\,,\,\beta\leftrightarrow\gamma\,,\,\dot{\beta}\leftrightarrow\dot{\gamma}\right)\\
&\phantom{=}+\frac{4iv^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\epsilon^{\dot{\beta}\dot{\gamma}}\epsilon^{\beta\gamma}}{m^{2}\omega_{0}\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,,\end{aligned}

(157)

where the frequency exchange is only present for manifest symmetry. In vectorial notation the last term coming from determinant insertion cancels and simplifies to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\!\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}(\omega_{0})\delta{z}^{\nu}(\omega_{1})\delta{z}^{\lambda}(\omega_{2})\rangle\!\rangle&=-\frac{4i}{m^{2}\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}\left[y_{0}^{\mu}(\eta^{\nu\lambda}+2v^{\nu}v^{\lambda})+v^{\mu}(v^{\nu}y_{0}^{\lambda}+y_{0}^{\nu}v^{\lambda})\right.\\
&\phantom{=}\left.\phantom{as}+i\epsilon^{\mu\nu}[v,y_{0}]v^{\lambda}+i\epsilon^{\mu\lambda}[v,y_{0}]v^{\nu}\right]\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,.\end{aligned}

(158)

A similar calculation for the conjugate 3pt correlator yields

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\!\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\omega_{0})\delta\bar{z}^{\dot{\beta}\beta}(\omega_{1})\delta{z}^{\dot{\gamma}\gamma}(\omega_{2})\rangle\!\rangle&=\frac{4iv^{\dot{\alpha}\gamma}}{m^{2}}\left(\frac{\epsilon^{\dot{\beta}\dot{\gamma}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{\omega_{0}\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}+\frac{2v^{\dot{\beta}\alpha}y_{0}^{\dot{\gamma}\beta}}{\omega_{0}\omega_{1}}\right)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\\
&\phantom{=asdf}+\left(\omega_{0}\leftrightarrow\omega_{1}\,,\,\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta\,,\,\dot{\alpha}\leftrightarrow\dot{\beta}\right)\\
&\phantom{=}+\frac{4iv^{\dot{\gamma}\gamma}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}}{m^{2}\omega_{0}\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,,\end{aligned}

(159)

which, in the vectorial notation, simplifies to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\!\langle\delta\bar{z}^{\mu}(\omega_{0})\delta\bar{z}^{\nu}(\omega_{1})\delta{z}^{\lambda}(\omega_{2})\rangle\!\rangle&=\frac{4i}{m^{2}\omega_{0}\omega_{1}}\left[y_{0}^{\lambda}(\eta^{\mu\nu}+2v^{\mu}v^{\nu})+v^{\lambda}(v^{\mu}y_{0}^{\nu}+y_{0}^{\mu}v^{\nu})\right.\\
&\phantom{=}\left.\phantom{as}-i\epsilon^{\lambda\mu}[v,y_{0}]v^{\nu}-i\epsilon^{\lambda\nu}[v,y_{0}]v^{\mu}\right]\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,.\end{aligned}

(160)

The following correlator may be of interest,

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\delta y^{\mu}(\omega_{0})\delta y^{\nu}(\omega_{1})\delta y^{\lambda}(\omega_{2})\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{m^{2}}\left[\frac{y^{\mu}(\eta^{\nu\lambda}+v^{\nu}v^{\lambda})}{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}+(\text{cyc.})\right]\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\,,

(161)

where
(\text{cyc.})
denotes cyclic permutation.

Note that
\delta z
and
\delta\bar{z}
variables do not obey Wick factorisation, e.g.
\langle(\delta z)(\delta\bar{z})(\delta\bar{z})\rangle\neq 0
. However, purely holomorphic/anti-holomorphic correlators such as
\langle(\delta z)(\delta z)\cdots(\delta z)\rangle
do obey Wick factorisation, since
\delta z
(
\delta\bar{z}
) is at most linear in
\mu
(
\bar{\mu}
) and the correlators reduce to the correlators of the form
\langle\mu\mu\cdots\mu\rangle
or
\langle\bar{\mu}\bar{\mu}\cdots\bar{\mu}\rangle
.

4.1.5
Photon coupling vertex rules

We begin with the spacetime picture where the vertex rules take a simple form that are easy to compare with other worldline models.
Inserting the mode expansion of fluctuations into the interaction (
44
), we get
8
8

8

Empty sum is zero and empty product is unity, i.e.
\sum_{i=1}^{0}\#=0
and
\prod_{i=1}^{0}\#=1
.

\displaystyle q\int_{k,\{\omega\}}A_{\mu}^{+}(k)(v^{\mu})e^{ik\cdot z_{0}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt\left((k\cdot v)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}(ik)\cdot\delta z(\omega_{i})

(162)

\displaystyle+q\int_{k,\{\omega\}}A_{\mu}^{+}(k)(-i\omega_{0})\delta z^{\mu}(\omega_{0})e^{ik\cdot z_{0}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt\left((k\cdot v)-\sum_{i=0}^{n}\omega_{i}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}(ik)\cdot\delta z(\omega_{i})

\displaystyle\phantom{asdf}+\left(A_{\mu}^{+}(k)\to A_{\mu}^{-}(k)\,,\,z_{0}^{\mu}\to\bar{z}_{0}^{\mu}\,,\,\delta z(\omega_{i})\to\delta\bar{z}(\omega_{i})\right)\,.

This expression is exact in
z_{0}
,
\bar{z}_{0}
. To obtain a result at a fixed order in the background spin-length
y_{0}
, we may simply set
z_{0}^{\mu}=b_{0}^{\mu}+iy_{0}^{\mu}
and
\bar{z}_{0}^{\mu}=b_{0}^{\mu}-iy_{0}^{\mu}
and expand in
y_{0}
.

To compute Compton amplitudes or the 2PL eikonal, we only need terms up to linear order in fluctuations,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} S_{\text{int}}&=q\int_{k}A_{\mu}^{+}(k)v^{\mu}e^{ik\cdot z_{0}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)\\
&\phantom{=}+iq\int_{k,\omega}A_{\mu}^{+}(k)\left[v^{\mu}k_{\nu}-\omega\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\right]\delta z^{\nu}(\omega)e^{ik\cdot z_{0}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt[(k\cdot v)-\omega]\\
&\phantom{asdf}+\left[A_{\mu}^{+}(k)\to A_{\mu}^{-}(k)\,,\,z_{0}^{\mu}\to\bar{z}_{0}^{\mu}\,,\,\delta z(\omega_{i})\to\delta\bar{z}(\omega_{i})\right]\,.\end{aligned}

(163)

Aside from the leading term proportional to
A^{\pm}\cdot v
, we can write the Feynman rules in terms of gauge-invariant field-strengths
F^{\pm}
, since the vertex rules with at least one
\delta z^{\mu}(\sigma)
fluctuation field
9
9

9

We argue using the time domain Feynman rules because the proof is simpler.
can be read out from the variational derivative

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{\text{int}}}{\delta[\delta z^{\mu}(\sigma)]}

\displaystyle=qF_{\mu\nu}^{+}[z_{0}+v\sigma+\delta z(\sigma)]\left(v^{\nu}+\frac{d(\delta z^{\nu}(\sigma))}{d\sigma}\right)\,,

(164)

which only depends on the field-strength
F^{+}
. The same argument trivially generalises to the anti-holomorphic sector. As a demonstration, we write the interaction terms up to quadratic order in worldline perturbations as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} S_{\text{int}}&=q\int_{k}(A_{k}^{+}\cdot v)e^{ik\cdot z_{0}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)+q\int_{k,\omega}(\delta z_{\omega}\cdot F_{k}^{+}\cdot v)e^{ik\cdot z_{0}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt[(k\cdot v)-\omega]\\
&\phantom{=}+\frac{q}{4}\int_{k,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}\hskip-15.0pt\left\{\left[(\delta z_{1}\cdot F^{+}_{k}\cdot v)(ik\cdot\delta z_{2})+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\right]+i(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})(\delta z_{1}\cdot F^{+}_{k}\cdot\delta z_{2})\right\}\\
&\phantom{=asdfasdfasdfasdf}\times e^{ik\cdot z_{0}}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt[(k\cdot v)-\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}]\\
&\phantom{asdf}+\left(A_{\mu}^{+}(k)\to A_{\mu}^{-}(k)\,,\,F_{\mu\nu}^{+}(k)\to F_{\mu\nu}^{-}(k)\,,\,z_{0}^{\mu}\to\bar{z}_{0}^{\mu}\,,\,\delta z(\omega_{i})\to\delta\bar{z}(\omega_{i})\right)\,.\end{aligned}

(165)

The expansion (
165
) is more useful than the expansion (
162
) since photon propagators can be chosen to be free of Dirac string singularities.
See section
4.2
for more discussions on the photon propagator.

4.2
Photon propagator

A worldline model of a charged particle
provides a localised source for the electromagnetic field.
Away from the sources, the photon propagates freely and the photon propagator is independent of the worldline model.
However, since the NJ shift forces us to separate the self-dual and anti-self-dual parts of the photon field,
we find it useful to recall some facts regarding how to split the propagator according to self-duality, which translates to the helicity of the photon at the quantum level.

In our twistor model, the photon field couples to the particle worldline via the NJ shift (
44
) which we copy here:

\displaystyle S_{\text{int}}=q\int A^{+}_{\mu}(z)dz^{\mu}+q\int A^{-}_{\mu}(\bar{z})d\bar{z}^{\mu}\,.

(166)

This coupling may look unfamiliar to the readers. To gain some intuition,
let us expand it to the quadratic order in
y
. The zeroth order term reproduces the standard minimal coupling for a non-spinning particle.
The linear order term is

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{int}}^{(1)}=q\int\left[\tilde{A}_{\mu}\dot{y}^{\mu}+y^{\mu}(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{A}_{\nu})\dot{x}^{\nu}\right]d\sigma\,.

(167)

The appearance of
\dot{y}
is a notable feature of the root-Kerr coupling. To linear order, we can remove it by integration by parts.
Up to a total derivative, we find

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{int}}^{(1)}=q\int\left[\tilde{F}_{\mu\nu}y^{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}\right]d\sigma=\frac{q}{2}\int\left[\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}y^{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}F^{\rho\sigma}\right]d\sigma\,.

(168)

Starting from the quadratic order, it is impossible to remove all
\dot{y}
factors.
Up to a total derivative, we find

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{int}}^{(2)}=\frac{q}{2}\int\left[\dot{y}^{\mu}y^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}-(\partial_{\mu}F_{\nu\rho})y^{\mu}y^{\nu}\dot{x}^{\rho}\right]d\sigma\,.

(169)

We can continue this expansion and express all
S^{(n)}_{\mathrm{int}}

(n\geq 1)
as Lorentz invariant products of
y^{\mu}
,
\dot{y}^{\mu}
,
\dot{x}^{\mu}
,
F_{\mu\nu}
and
\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
, with no reference to
A^{\pm}_{\mu}
at all.
With this form of the action, the usual propagator for the photon field will suffice for all perturbative computations.

The beauty of the NJ shift (
166
) is that we can perform computations exactly in
y
without ever expanding in powers of
y
.
A small price to pay is that we should use less familiar propagators written in terms of
A^{\pm}_{\mu}
.

Our discussion is inspired by Zwanziger’s (electromagnetic-duality covariant) two-potential formalism
Zwanziger:1970hk
(see also refs.
Gubarev:1998ss
;
Shnir:2011zz
;
Terning:2020dzg
;
Moynihan:2020gxj
).
But, we will not directly follow Zwanziger’s formalism in that we never use two potentials or consider sources with net magnetic charges.
We are interested in the long-distance interaction between two spatially localised sources.
The interaction is captured by the integral,

\displaystyle I_{12}=\int J_{1}^{\mu}(x)\langle A_{\mu}(x)A_{\nu}(y)\rangle J_{2}^{\nu}(y)\,.

(170)

We are doing classical physics, but we can use the propagator (Green’s function) in a QFT notation, where
\langle A_{\mu}A_{\nu}\rangle
is the 2-point function, which we take to be time-ordered for concreteness.

Let us temporarily ignore the net (electric or magnetic) charges and focus on the dipole or higher multipole moments.
For a magnetic dipole, it is well known that a long-distance observer cannot distinguish an
Ampèrian dipole (electric current loop) from a Gilbertian dipole (two opposing magnetic monopole charges).
A similar story holds for an electric dipole and all higher electric/magnetic multipole moments.
So, as far as the long-distance interaction is concerned, we can describe the
same
source using
either
an electric current
or
a magnetic current.

To switch between the two pictures, we recall that
Maxwell’s equations with both electric and magnetic sources read

\displaystyle d^{\dagger}F=J\,,\quad d^{\dagger}({\smash{{*}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003ptF}\vphantom{F}})={\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}\,,\quad d^{\dagger}F:=(\partial^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu})\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}\,.

(171)

Electric-magnetic duality states that
this set of equations is invariant under

\displaystyle\text{EMD}\quad:\quad\,F\,\,\mapsto\,\,{\smash{{*}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003ptF}\vphantom{F}}\,,\quad(J,{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}})\,\,\mapsto\,\,({\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}},-J)\,.

(172)

It is natural to use the complex combinations of
F
and
{\smash{{*}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003ptF}\vphantom{F}}
that are eigenstates of
*
,

\displaystyle F^{\pm}:={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\Big({F\pm i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{{*}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003ptF}\vphantom{F}}}\Big)\,,\quad J^{\pm}:={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\Big({J\pm i{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}}\Big)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad d^{\dagger}F^{\pm}=J^{\pm}\,.

(173)

For a given multipole, in the electric picture, we solve

\displaystyle d^{\dagger}F=J\,,\quad d^{\dagger}({\smash{{*}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003ptF}\vphantom{F}})=0\,,

(174)

while in the magnetic picture, we solve

\displaystyle d^{\dagger}F=0\,,\quad d^{\dagger}({\smash{{*}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003ptF}\vphantom{F}})={\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}\,.

(175)

The two pictures are related such that
F
away from the source is exactly the same.
In other words, for a “point-like” source, the difference between the two pictures
is ultra-local (delta function supported).

Depending on which picture we choose for each of the two sources,
the integral (
170
) can take different forms,

\displaystyle\begin{split}I_{12}&=\int J_{1}(x)\langle A(x)A(y)\rangle J_{2}(y)=\int{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{1}(x)\langle A^{\star}(x)A^{\star}(y)\rangle{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{2}(y)\\
&=\int J_{1}(x)\langle A(x)A^{\star}(y)\rangle{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{2}(y)=\int{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{1}(x)\langle A^{\star}(x)A(y)\rangle J_{2}(y)\,,\end{split}

(176)

where we suppressed the vector indices to avoid clutter. If we call
\langle AA\rangle
“electric-electric” propagator,
we may call
\langle A^{\star}A^{\star}\rangle
“magnetic-magnetic”,

\langle AA^{\star}\rangle
‘‘electric-magnetic”, etc.
In the QFT approach to the propagators, which we will review shortly,
we split the mode expansion according to the photon’s helicity such that
10
10

10

The notation of this section is related to those in appendix
A
as
\tilde{A}=-A^{\star}
. The minus sign originates from
{{*}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt}F=-*F
.

\displaystyle A=A^{+}+A^{-}\,,\quad A^{\star}=-i(A^{+}-A^{-})\,.

(177)

Since
\langle A^{+}A^{+}\rangle=0=\langle A^{-}A^{-}\rangle
, it follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle AA\rangle=\langle A^{+}A^{-}\rangle+\langle A^{-}A^{+}\rangle=\langle A^{\star}A^{\star}\rangle\,,\\
&\langle AA^{\star}\rangle=+i\left[\langle A^{+}A^{-}\rangle-\langle A^{-}A^{+}\rangle\right]=-\langle A^{\star}A\rangle\,.\end{split}

(178)

So far, our discussion has been general. Now let us focus on the multipole moments of a root-Kerr particle.
As we saw in (
168
) and (
169
), we find electric moments at
\mathcal{O}(y^{2k})

and magnetic moments at
\mathcal{O}(y^{2k+1})
.
This splitting is expected to be a generic feature of any parity-preserving spinning charged particle.
The NJ shift (
166
) suggests a hybrid approach
which uses the electric picture for the electric multipoles and the magnetic picture of the magnetic multipoles.
We denote the currents by
J_{e}
and
{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{o}
where
e
and
o
stand for even and odd, respectively.

Contributions from different multipole moments simply add up to give

\displaystyle\begin{split}I_{12}&=\int J_{1,e}(x)\langle A(x)A(y)\rangle J_{2,e}(y)+\int{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{1,o}(x)\langle A^{\star}(x)A^{\star}(y)\rangle{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{2,o}(y)\\
&\quad+\int J_{1,e}(x)\langle A(x)A^{\star}(y)\rangle{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{2,o}(y)+\int{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{1,o}(x)\langle A^{\star}(x)A(y)\rangle J_{2,e}(y)\,.\end{split}

(179)

Rewriting it in terms of
\langle A^{\pm}A^{\mp}\rangle
, we find

\displaystyle I_{12}=\int J_{1,+}(x)\langle A^{+}(x)A^{-}(y)\rangle J_{2,-}(y)+\int J_{1,-}(x)\langle A^{-}(x)A^{+}(y)\rangle J_{2,+}\,,

(180)

where, for each source,

\displaystyle J_{\pm}=J_{e}\mp i{\smash{J^{\star}}\vphantom{J}}_{o}\,.

(181)

Comparing this with (
173
), we note a slightly non-trivial “metric” in the complex basis,

\displaystyle J_{\pm}=2J^{\mp}\,.

(182)

Applying it to the root-Kerr particle, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}(J_{-})^{\mu}(x)=(J^{+})^{\mu}(x)&=\frac{1}{2}q\int\dot{\bar{z}}^{\mu}(\sigma)\delta^{4}(x-\bar{z}(\sigma))d\sigma\,,\\
\frac{1}{2}(J_{+})^{\mu}(x)=(J^{-})^{\mu}(x)&=\frac{1}{2}q\int\dot{z}^{\mu}(\sigma)\delta^{4}(x-z(\sigma))d\sigma\,.\end{split}

(183)

To summarize, we took a long route to explain how the NJ shift (
166
) can be understood in conventional descriptions of multipole moments, only to motivate a less familiar method;
the most efficient way to compute the interaction between two root-Kerr particles
is to use the “helicity propagators”
\langle A^{\pm}A^{\mp}\rangle
.

4.2.1
Helicity propagator

Let us present the result first and review the derivation.
In terms of self-dual and anti-self-dual fields,
the propagators are
Weinberg:1965rz

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta^{+-}_{\mu\nu}(k)&:=\langle A_{\mu}^{+}(k)A_{\nu}^{-}(-k)\rangle=\frac{i}{k^{2}-i0^{+}}\left[\frac{2k_{(\mu}n_{\nu)}-(k\cdot n)\eta_{\mu\nu}+i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}k^{\alpha}n^{\beta}}{2(k\cdot n)}\right]\,,\\
\Delta^{-+}_{\mu\nu}(k)&=\Delta^{+-}_{\nu\mu}(k)=[\Delta^{+-}_{\mu\nu}(k)]^{*}\,,\quad\Delta^{++}_{\mu\nu}(k)=\Delta^{--}_{\mu\nu}(k)=0\,,\end{split}

(184)

where
n_{\mu}
is an auxiliary reference vector,
which we call a “Dirac string”.
The spinor notation offers a more compact expression.
Pictorially, we denote the propagator as

\fmffile{photon-propagator}\parbox{80.0pt}{\fmfgraph*(50,30)
\fmfstraight\fmfleft{i1,o1}
\fmfright{i2,o2}
\fmf{phantom}{i1,v1,o1}
\fmf{phantom}{i2,v2,o2}
\fmffreeze\fmf{wiggly,label=$k$,tension=0}{v1,v2}
\fmfv{decor.shape=square,decor.filled=empty,decor.size=10,
label=$A^{+}_{{\smash{\alpha{\dot{\alpha}}}\vphantom{\beta}}}\;$,label.angle=180}{v1}
\fmfv{decor.shape=square,decor.filled=shaded,decor.size=10,
label=$\;A^{-}_{\smash{\beta{\dot{\beta}}}\vphantom{\beta}}$,label.angle=0}{v2}
}\;=\;\Delta^{+-}_{{\smash{\alpha{\dot{\alpha}}\beta{\dot{\beta}}}\vphantom{\beta}}}\hskip-0.50003pt(k)\;=\;\frac{n_{{\smash{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}\vphantom{\beta}}}\hskip 0.24994ptk_{{\smash{\beta{\dot{\alpha}}}\vphantom{\beta}}}}{n{\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}k}\frac{i}{k^{2}-i0^{+}}\,.

(185)

For later purposes, we also note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta^{+-}_{\mu\nu}(k)+\Delta^{-+}_{\mu\nu}(k)&=\frac{-i}{k^{2}-i0^{+}}\left(\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{2k_{(\mu}n_{\nu)}}{k\cdot n}\right)\,,\\
i\Delta^{+-}_{\mu\nu}(k)-i\Delta^{-+}_{\mu\nu}(k)&=\frac{-i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}k^{\alpha}n^{\beta}}{(k^{2}-i0^{+})(k\cdot n)}\,.\end{split}

(186)

The chiral photon field
A_{\mu}^{\pm}
frequently appears in the form of the field strength tensor
F_{\mu\nu}^{\pm}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}^{\pm}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}^{\pm}
. We consider the combinations

\displaystyle\langle F_{\mu\nu}^{\pm}(k)A_{\lambda}^{\mp}(-k)\rangle v^{\lambda}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)\,,\quad\langle F_{\mu\nu}^{\pm}(k)F_{\lambda\sigma}^{\mp}(-k)\rangle\,.

These 2pt functions can be expressed without the auxiliary reference vector
n_{\mu}
, since they can be constructed from the non-chiral photon propagator (
186
) using the (anti-)self-dual projectors

\displaystyle(P^{\pm})_{\mu\nu}^{\alpha\beta}:=\frac{\delta_{\mu}^{\alpha}\delta_{\nu}^{\beta}-\delta_{\mu}^{\beta}\delta_{\nu}^{\alpha}\mp i\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{\phantom{\mu\nu}\alpha\beta}}{4}\,,\quad F_{\mu\nu}^{\pm}=(P^{\pm})_{\mu\nu}^{\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\beta}=:(P^{\pm}\cdot F)_{\mu\nu}\,.

(187)

resulting in

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle F^{\pm}_{\mu\nu}(k)A^{\mp}_{\alpha}(-k)\rangle v^{\alpha}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)&=(P^{\pm})_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda\sigma}\langle F_{\lambda\sigma}(k)A_{\alpha}(-k)\rangle\rangle v^{\alpha}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)\\
&=\frac{k_{\mu}v_{\nu}-v_{\mu}k_{\nu}\mp i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}k^{\alpha}v^{\beta}}{2(k^{2}-i0^{+})}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)\,,\end{aligned}

(188)

and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle F^{+}_{\mu\nu}(k)F^{-}_{\alpha\beta}(-k)\rangle&=(P^{+})_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda\sigma}(P^{-})_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma\delta}\langle F_{\lambda\sigma}(k)F_{\gamma\delta}(-k)\rangle\\
&=\frac{-i[\eta_{\mu\alpha}k_{\nu}k_{\beta}-\eta_{\nu\alpha}k_{\mu}k_{\beta}-\eta_{\mu\beta}k_{\nu}k_{\alpha}+\eta_{\nu\beta}k_{\mu}k_{\alpha}]}{2(k^{2}-i0^{+})}\\
&\phantom{=as}+\frac{-\left(k_{\mu}\epsilon_{\nu\alpha\beta\lambda}-k_{\nu}\epsilon_{\mu\alpha\beta\lambda}\right)k^{\lambda}+\left(k_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta\mu\nu\lambda}-k_{\beta}\epsilon_{\alpha\mu\nu\lambda}\right)k^{\lambda}}{4(k^{2}-i0^{+})}\,,\end{aligned}

(189)

where we have dropped the ultra-local (non-pole-possessing) terms. These 2pt functions can also be derived from mode expansions of chiral photon fields.

We remark that all scattering observables and the classical eikonal (except for the 1PL eikonal) can be computed from the 2pt functions (
188
) and (
189
), therefore the dependence on the “Dirac string” of (
184
) is only superficial.

4.2.2
Mode expansion for the propagator

We can obtain the helicity propagator (
184
) through an off-shell extension of the on-shell mode expansion in QFT.
We define the polarisation vectors as (
k^{0}>0,n^{0}>0
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \varepsilon_{\mu}^{\pm}(+k^{0},\vec{k})=\varepsilon_{\mu}^{\pm}(\vec{k})\,,&\quad\varepsilon_{\mu}^{\pm}(-k^{0},\vec{k})=-\varepsilon_{\mu}^{\pm}(-\vec{k})\,,\\
\varepsilon_{\mu}^{+}(k)=\frac{[k|\sigma_{\mu}|n\rangle}{\sqrt{2}\langle kn\rangle}\,,&\quad\varepsilon_{\mu}^{-}(k)=\frac{\langle k|\sigma_{\mu}|n]}{\sqrt{2}[kn]}\,,\end{aligned}

(190)

such that
[\varepsilon_{\mu}^{\pm}(k)]^{\ast}=\varepsilon_{\mu}^{\mp}(-k)
. The mode expansion of the chiral photon fields
A_{\mu}^{\pm}(x)
are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A_{\mu}^{\pm}(x)&=\int_{\vec{k}}\frac{1}{2k^{0}}\left[\varepsilon_{\mu}^{\pm}(\vec{k})a_{\vec{k},\pm}e^{-ik^{0}t+i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}+\left[\varepsilon_{\mu}^{\mp}(\vec{k})\right]^{\ast}(a_{\vec{k},\mp})^{\dagger}e^{+ik^{0}t-i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\right]\\
&=\int_{\vec{k}}\frac{\varepsilon_{\mu}^{\pm}(k)}{2k^{0}}\left[a_{\vec{k},\pm}e^{-ik^{0}t+i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}-(a_{\vec{k},\mp})^{\dagger}e^{+ik^{0}t-i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\right]\,,\end{aligned}

(191)

where
k^{0}=|\vec{k}|
and
[A_{\mu}^{\pm}(x)]^{\dagger}=A_{\mu}^{\mp}(x)
. The creation-annihilation operators satisfy

\displaystyle\left[a_{\vec{k},h},(a_{\vec{k}^{\prime},h^{\prime}})^{\dagger}\right]=2k^{0}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt^{(3)}(\vec{k}-\vec{k}^{\prime})\delta_{h,h^{\prime}}\,,\quad h,h^{\prime}=\pm\,.

(192)

The usual photon field and the dual photon field are given as (see appendix
A
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A_{\mu}(x)&=A_{\mu}^{+}(x)+A_{\mu}^{-}(x)\,,\,\\
\tilde{A}_{\mu}(x)&=iA_{\mu}^{+}(x)-iA_{\mu}^{-}(x)\,.\end{aligned}

(193)

The time-ordered 2pt functions are

\displaystyle\langle A_{\mu}^{\pm}(x)A_{\nu}^{\pm}(0)\rangle

\displaystyle=0\,,

(194)

\displaystyle\langle A_{\mu}^{+}(x)A_{\nu}^{-}(0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\vec{k}}\frac{\varepsilon_{\mu}^{+}(\vec{k})[\varepsilon_{\nu}^{+}(\vec{k})]^{\ast}\Theta(t)e^{-i|\vec{k}|t+i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}+[\varepsilon_{\mu}^{-}(\vec{k})]^{\ast}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}(\vec{k})\Theta(-t)e^{+i|\vec{k}|t-i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}{2|\vec{k}|}

\displaystyle=\int_{\vec{k}}\frac{\Theta(t)e^{-i|\vec{k}|t+i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}+\Theta(-t)e^{+i|\vec{k}|t-i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}{2|\vec{k}|}\times\left[-\varepsilon_{\mu}^{+}(k)\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}(k)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{k^{\mu}}\frac{i[\varepsilon_{\mu}^{+}(k)\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}(k)]}{k^{2}-i0^{+}}e^{ik\cdot x}

\displaystyle=\int_{k^{\mu}}\frac{i}{k^{2}-i0^{+}}\left[\frac{2k_{(\mu}n_{\nu)}-(k\cdot n)\eta_{\mu\nu}+i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}k^{\alpha}n^{\beta}}{2(n\cdot k)}\right]e^{ik\cdot x}\,,

(195)

where we used the identity

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{-\omega^{2}+\vec{k}^{2}-i0^{+}}&=\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{(|\vec{k}|-i0^{+}-\omega)(|\vec{k}|-i0^{+}+\omega)}\\
&=\frac{+i}{2|\vec{k}|}\left[\Theta(t)e^{-i|\vec{k}|t}+\Theta(-t)e^{+i|\vec{k}|t}\right]\,.\end{aligned}

(196)

The computation can be repeated for 2pt functions of field strength tensors, which can be used to justify the propagators (
188
) and (
189
). For example, (
188
) can be computed from the substitution

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu}^{+}(k)\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}(k)\to 2i[k_{[\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu]}^{+}(k)\varepsilon_{\lambda}^{-}(k)]v^{\lambda}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)=\frac{ik_{[\mu}[k|\sigma_{\nu]}|n\rangle[n|v|k\rangle}{\langle kn\rangle[nk]}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)\,,

in the second line of (
195
) before using the identity (
196
), where
k^{\mu}=(|\vec{k}|,\vec{k})
satisfies the on-shell condition
k^{2}=0
. Using the delta constraint, we can recast the numerator as

\displaystyle\frac{ik_{[\mu}[k|\sigma_{\nu]}|n\rangle[n|v|k\rangle}{\langle kn\rangle[nk]}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{4}[k|\sigma_{\mu}\sigma_{\nu}v|k\rangle\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}\left(k_{\mu}v_{\nu}-k_{\nu}v_{\mu}-i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}k^{\alpha}v^{\beta}\right)\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)\,,

which leads to the 2pt function (
188
) after off-shell continuation
k^{2}\neq 0
using (
196
).

With the modified definitions for the mode operators (
k^{0}>0
)

\displaystyle a_{k^{\mu},\pm}=a_{\vec{k},\pm}\,,\,a_{-k^{\mu},\pm}=-a_{-\vec{k},\mp}^{\dagger}\,,\,k^{\mu}=(k^{0},\vec{k})\,,

(197)

we can rewrite the mode expansion (
191
) as

\displaystyle A_{\mu}^{\pm}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{k}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k^{2})\,\varepsilon_{\mu}^{\pm}(k)\,a_{k,\pm}\,e^{ik\cdot x}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{off-shell}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad\int_{k}A_{\mu}^{\pm}(k)\,e^{ik\cdot x}\,,

(198)

which is the off-shell continued form used to obtain Feynman rules.

5
Compton amplitude

In this section, we compute the classical Compton amplitudes for a root-Kerr particle,
and compare them with similar results in the literature.
We find perfect agreement to the linear order in spin (at
g=2
), whereas we find model-dependent discrepancy starting from
the quadratic order in spin.

3-point coupling

The shift (
166
) induces the 3-point coupling of an incoming positive helicity photon,

\displaystyle iA_{3}\sim iq(\varepsilon^{+}\cdot v)e^{-k\cdot y}e^{ik\cdot b}\delta\kern-1.99997pt\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\bar{}$}\kern 1.99997pt(k\cdot v)\,,

(199)

where
k^{\mu}
is photon momentum.
This agrees with the black hole 3-point coupling in the literature. For example, appendix B. of
Chen:2021kxt
gives the minimal coupling as

\displaystyle M_{3}^{\eta,s}

\displaystyle=M_{3}^{\eta,s=0}\times\exp[-\eta\kappa_{0}(k\cdot a)]\,,

(200)

where
\eta=\pm 1
is the helicity sign of the incoming massless quanta of momentum
k^{\mu}
,
a^{\mu}
is the spin-length vector
(y^{\mu}=-a^{\mu})
, and
\kappa_{0}=\eta_{00}
is the metric convention parameter.

5.1
Computation

The Compton amplitudes will first be computed using the Feynman rules derived from the interaction term expansion (
162
) to parallel the WQFT computations in the literature
Wang:2022ntx
;
Kim:2023drc
, which will be reorganised into a form that connects more naturally to the Feynman rules of the alternative expansion (
165
).
